**Assistant — Turn 38**

Episodes 3–4 are an important pivot because Season 2 stops being only about “can Liella! recruit new first-years?” and becomes a more layered question:

> **What does Liella!’s light look like from the outside — as inspiration, as intimidation, as pressure, as rivalry, or as a place for people who think they do not belong?**

Episode 3 introduces the external competitive standard through Wien Margarete. Episode 4 brings the internal recruitment question back through Mei and Shiki. Together, they sharpen Season 2’s central tension: Liella! must be serious enough to become champions, but open enough that people who feel unqualified can still enter.

# Episode 3: favorites to win, but not winners yet

Episode 3 is titled **“Favorites to Win,”** and the title is almost cruel. Liella! is invited back to the Yoyogi School Idol Festival as the closing act, which means the outside world now treats them as a major group. They are no longer the newcomers struggling for permission to exist. They are expected to perform like a leading contender.

But the episode immediately exposes the uncomfortable truth:

> Liella! has reputation before achievement.

They have attention.
They have admiration.
They have school support.
They have Sunny Passion’s praise.
But they still have not actually won a major competition.

That creates the central anxiety of the episode. They are being treated as “favorites,” yet internally they know their record does not justify that status. This is especially important for Kanon, because Season 1 ended with her learning the pain of losing after everyone supported them. Episode 3 makes that pressure explicit. She wants to live up to the expectations of Sunny Passion, Yuigaoka, and everyone who now sees Liella! as the school’s pride.

## Sunny Passion’s ambition

The conversation with Sunny Passion is doing important work. Kanon asks why they praised Liella! so highly, and Sunny Passion answers that Liella! really did move them, especially on the Christmas stage. But they also make clear that they still intend to win again.

That matters because Sunny Passion are not just benevolent senpai. They are generous, but they are still competitors. Their desire to win back-to-back and carve their school and island into Love Live history gives them a scale of ambition Liella! still lacks.

Kanon’s instinct is still a little too pure: she can admire Sunny Passion’s dream and cheer them on, even though their victory would mean Liella!’s defeat. Sunny Passion gently points this out. This continues the Season 1 finale’s lesson: competition is not only about beautiful feelings. If everyone’s dreams matter, then victory also means denying someone else’s dream.

That is a hard truth for Kanon, because her leadership is built around making room for others. Love Live asks her to accept that making room is not enough. At some point, Liella! must take space.

## Wien Margarete as anti-Liella

Wien Margarete’s entrance is one of the sharpest antagonistic introductions in *Superstar!!* so far. She does not approach Liella! as a fan, a prospective member, or a supportive rival. She approaches Kanon almost like a talent scout or judge:

> “You’re one of the favorites to win, right? Then sing for me.”

That is a direct challenge to Kanon’s Season 1 recovery. Kanon learned that her voice could exist in relation — with Keke, Chisato, Liella!, the school, the audience. Wien isolates the voice again. She looks at Kanon and says, in effect:

> Prove your individual worth.

This is why Wien is immediately threatening. She does not merely want to beat Liella!. She implicitly rejects the collective school-idol logic that has saved Kanon.

Her song, “Butterfly Wing,” reinforces this. The lyrics are full of solitary struggle, enemies, self-polishing, flight without wings, and the idea that only those with fervent desire deserve to be chosen. That is almost the opposite of Liella!’s “connected lights” philosophy. Wien’s world is vertical, selective, aristocratic, and individual. Liella!’s world is horizontal, relational, and communal.

Wien’s challenge is therefore philosophical:

> **Is school idol greatness born from shared connection, or from solitary exceptional talent?**

Liella! loses badly enough that this question cannot be dismissed.

## Liella!’s second Yoyogi failure

Liella! receives honorable mention again. From the outside, that is not shameful. From their own perspective, it is devastating. They entered as the favorites and lost to a middle-schooler.

Kinako blames herself because she is the new, least experienced member. This is exactly the Season 2 problem made painful: if Liella! includes beginners, then those beginners will fear they are holding the group back. Sumire’s answer is important: they were all up there together, so the result belongs to all of them. That is school-idol ethics at its best. It protects Kinako from becoming the scapegoat.

But Wien then worsens the wound by dismissing Love Live itself. If Liella! are supposed favorites, then the competition must be easy. She even says Kanon at least has talent “for singing,” separating Kanon from the group as if the others are just dead weight.

This is a dangerous temptation for Kanon’s story. Season 1 made her the emotional center. Wien tries to convert her into an isolated star.

## Yuigaoka’s response: pride without complacency

The Yuigaoka students’ response is the emotional corrective. They congratulate Liella! for the honorable mention, call them the school’s pride and joy, and tell them to keep aiming to win.

This could have been written as empty consolation, but it lands differently because of the school’s history. Yuigaoka is still young. Its club accomplishments are sparse. Liella!’s achievements matter not because they are trophies, but because they give the school a shared identity.

The key phrase is **“Superstar!!”** The students call Liella! their superstars not because they have already become national champions, but because they have made Yuigaoka feel like a school with a future.

That is a crucial distinction:

> Winning gives external proof.
> Liella! has already given internal pride.

But the episode does not let them stop there. The students’ pride becomes not a permission to relax, but a reason to continue.

## “We’re all the center”

Kanon’s choice to perform without a center is the episode’s strongest answer to Wien. Wien isolates Kanon’s individual singing talent. Kanon responds by refusing a hierarchy inside Liella!.

When she says they are all the center — and that Yuigaoka’s students and the school itself are also center — she is expanding the idea of performance beyond individual prominence. This is not the same as saying individual talent does not matter. Kanon’s singing still matters enormously. But the meaning of the performance comes from the whole field around them.

“Go!! Restart” is therefore not just a recovery song after defeat. It is Liella!’s answer to the Yoyogi loss:

> We failed again, but this pain belongs to all of us.
> So does the restart.

This is also where Kinako is fully absorbed into Liella!’s emotional logic. Her weakness does not make the group less Liella!. The group’s willingness to carry weakness together is part of what makes them Liella!.

# Episode 4: Mei and Shiki, or the girls who think they are not idol-shaped

Episode 4 is titled **“The Two in the Science Room,”** and it returns Season 2 to the recruitment problem. But it is doing something more specific than Episode 1–2. Kinako’s issue was ordinary inadequacy: she admired Liella! but feared she lacked ability. Mei and Shiki’s issue is different:

> They both want connection, but each believes her own nature makes her unsuitable for it.

This is why the episode pairs the school idol club presidency plot with the Mei/Shiki plot. Both are about people saying, “I am not cut out for that,” when the real issue is fear.

## Kanon refuses formal centrality

The episode begins with the question of club president. Everyone assumes Kanon is the obvious choice because she started the group emotionally, brought everyone together, and remains Liella!’s interpretive center. Kinako, especially, sees her as the natural president.

But Kanon hesitates. Her reasoning is extremely important:

> Since they are trying to reinvent themselves as a group, the president should be someone else.

This is one of Kanon’s most mature decisions so far. She recognizes that if Liella! is to become more than the original Kanon-centered miracle, its formal structure cannot simply reproduce Season 1. She was the center of formation, but Season 2 requires distributed leadership.

Her suggestion of Chisato is also meaningful. Chisato has always been the person who gives the group bodily structure: training, rhythm, discipline, choreography, steadiness. Making Chisato president formalizes what she has already been doing.

So Kanon is not abdicating responsibility. She is refusing to let the group’s future collapse into her own centrality.

## Chisato as president

Chisato accepting the presidency is a quiet but major evolution. In Season 1, her wound was that she believed she needed to become strong alone before standing beside Kanon. Now she accepts a role where she must support everyone openly, not as hidden helper but as formal leader.

That is a nice continuation of her arc:

Season 1 Chisato: “I want to become strong enough to support Kanon.”
Season 2 Chisato: “I want to support this whole group as president.”

She is still nervous and says she may not be cut out for it, but she chooses to try. That choice mirrors what the episode later asks of Mei and Shiki.

The show is drawing a line between three “not cut out” claims:

-   Kanon: not cut out to be president.

-   Chisato: not cut out to be president.

-   Mei/Shiki: not cut out to be school idols.


The answer is not that everyone is secretly perfectly suited. The answer is that “not cut out” is often what fear sounds like before effort begins.

# Mei Yoneme: the hidden fan

Mei’s characterization becomes much sharper here. She is not merely a delinquent-looking girl with a temper. She is a painfully self-conscious school idol fan whose face, poor eyesight, and social defensiveness have made her seem hostile when she is actually overwhelmed.

The reveal of her room is very strong: Liella! poster, rare school idol media, collectibles, and intense fan knowledge. Keke’s reaction to the rare DVD is funny, but it also confirms that Mei is not casually interested. She is deeply invested.

That changes every previous Mei scene. Her aggression was not indifference. It was embarrassment.

Her core wound is:

> **She loves school idols, but cannot imagine herself as someone allowed to be one.**

She gives several reasons: her face, her personality, her inability to smile. These are not trivial insecurities. For a school idol, “smile” is almost ideological. To say “I can’t even put on a smile” is to say “I lack the basic expressive language this world requires.”

That makes Mei a strong addition to *Superstar!!*’s cast. She is not blocked by lack of talent exactly. She is blocked by self-image. She believes her outer presentation disqualifies her inner desire.

## Mei and Kanon

Kanon recognizes Mei’s “not cut out” logic because she has heard versions of it all season:

Kanon: I can’t sing.
Sumire: I’ll never be chosen.
Chisato: I can’t stand beside Kanon unless I prove myself.
Ren: I cannot repeat my mother’s school idol memories.
Kinako: I cannot keep up.
Mei: I cannot be a school idol because of my face/personality.

Kanon’s great strength is that she hears the desire underneath the refusal. With Mei, she does not argue “you are obviously idol material.” That would not work. She instead points to Mei’s loneliness and embarrassment, then connects it to Shiki.

Her key insight is that Mei and Shiki are alike:

> Both are easily embarrassed.
> Both feel lonely.
> Both hate that vulnerability.
> Both pretend they would rather be alone.

That is not just recruitment rhetoric. It is a correct diagnosis of their relationship.

# Shiki Wakana: the loner who engineers connection

Shiki initially appears as a deadpan science eccentric: microphones, devices, experiments, emotional flatness, practical observation. But Episode 4 reveals that her detachment is another form of loneliness.

Her backstory with Mei is important. Shiki says she was always alone and fine with that. Mei reached out to her, kept showing up, and eventually even joined the science club despite not caring about science. Shiki’s science room is therefore not simply her eccentric domain. It is the place Mei chose to remain with her.

This is why Shiki’s attempt to push Mei toward the school idol club hurts so much. On the surface, Shiki is sacrificing her own companionship so Mei can pursue her real dream. Emotionally, she is also denying that she needs Mei.

Her cruel line — telling Mei not to come to the science room if she is not really interested — is an attempt to cut the knot cleanly. She thinks she is freeing Mei. But because Mei’s presence is itself an act of care, Shiki is also rejecting the relationship that helped her stop being alone.

Shiki’s core wound:

> **She does not understand why someone would choose to stay with her, so she tries to release that person before becoming a burden.**

That is different from Mei, but complementary.

Mei thinks, “I am not idol material.”
Shiki thinks, “I am not someone worth staying for.”

The episode resolves both by making them admit that they can try only if they try together.

# Mei and Shiki as a pair

This is one of the strongest relationship constructions among the new first-years because neither is simply “saved” by the other. They have been mutually distorting themselves for each other.

Mei joined the science club to stay with Shiki, even though she wanted school idols.
Shiki tried to push Mei away because she thought Mei was sacrificing too much.
Mei interpreted Shiki’s self-erasure as rejection.
Shiki interpreted Mei’s loyalty as obligation.

The truth is simpler and more vulnerable:

> Mei wants school idols, but she also wants Shiki.
> Shiki says she wants to be alone, but she wants Mei.

Their shared confession is deliberately awkward. Mei says she does not really like school idols. Shiki points out that her face is red. Shiki says she cannot smile. Mei tells her she is cute. Shiki replies that Mei is cuter. It is comic, but it is also the first time they stop translating affection into avoidance.

The decisive line is:

> “I think I could try my best if you were there with me.”

That is very *Superstar!!*. Just as Kanon could sing because she was not alone, Mei and Shiki can try school idols because neither has to perform an acceptable self alone.

# Kanon Shibuya: updated profile through S2E4

Kanon’s Season 2 leadership is becoming less about rescuing people directly and more about **creating structures where other people can choose each other**.

In Episode 3, she responds to Wien’s isolating challenge by declaring that everyone is center. In Episode 4, she refuses to become club president and supports Chisato taking the role. Then she helps Mei and Shiki see that their relationship is not an obstacle to school idols, but the very condition that makes school idols possible for them.

This is a subtle but important evolution.

Season 1 Kanon often solved the emotional problem personally.
Season 2 Kanon increasingly distributes agency.

She does not become president.
She does not make herself center.
She does not recruit Mei alone.
She recognizes the bond between Mei and Shiki and lets that bond become the bridge.

That is a more mature form of leadership.

## Kanon versus Wien

Wien also opens a potential new pressure point for Kanon. Kanon has recovered from stage fright, but Wien’s attention singles out her singing talent in a way that could separate her from the group. This is dangerous because Kanon’s identity has been rebuilt relationally. If Wien keeps framing Kanon as the only truly talented one, Kanon may have to confront the question:

> Can she accept her individual excellence without letting it fracture Liella!’s collective identity?

That is probably the next major test for her.

# Chisato: updated profile

Chisato becoming club president is one of the cleanest continuations of her Season 1 arc.

She is no longer just Kanon’s childhood friend, dance coach, or emotional support. She is now the formal organizer of Liella!’s club identity. This is especially fitting because Season 2’s core problem is integration. Kanon is the emotional interpreter, but Chisato may be better suited to daily structure, training, and group balance.

Her presidency also gently decentralizes Kanon. If Kanon remains the emotional center, Chisato becomes the operational center.

That gives Liella! a healthier structure:

-   Kanon: voice, interpretation, emotional direction.

-   Chisato: training, organization, bodily/group cohesion.

-   Ren: institutional connection to the student council and Yuigaoka.

-   Keke: idol faith and dream pressure.

-   Sumire: presentation, showbiz realism, friction.

-   Kinako: beginner’s perspective.

-   Mei/Shiki: concealed desire and mutual dependence entering the group.


# Kinako after S2E4

Kinako continues to function as the bridge between Liella! and ordinary first-years. She is the one who talks about school idols in class, invites classmates to the roof, and accidentally creates recruitment openings. She is also still visibly new enough that Mei and Shiki can see joining as less impossible.

That matters. If only the original five were recruiting, Mei might continue to see Liella! as unreachable. Kinako’s presence proves that a first-year can enter.

Her function is not simply “sixth member.” She is the proof of permeability.

# Natsumi in the background

Natsumi’s brief presence in Episode 4 is useful foreshadowing. She appears exhausted from work, filming, editing, and monetization. While Mei and Shiki are dealing with the problem of belonging, Natsumi is already marked by a different problem:

> performance as labor, content, and money.

The next episode title makes that obvious, but even here she feels like the member whose relationship to performance will be least “pure” in the Love Live sense. That could be valuable, because Season 2 is already dealing with the difference between inspiration, reputation, and results. Natsumi may add the question of value in the economic/media sense.

# Liella! after S2E4

By the end of Episode 4, Liella! has eight members, but more importantly, the group’s structure has changed.

Season 1 Liella! was a five-member unit built around recovered dreams.
Early Season 2 Liella! is becoming a school club that can absorb very different kinds of first-years.

Kinako enters through ordinary admiration and effort.
Mei enters through hidden fandom and shame.
Shiki enters through loneliness, science-room attachment, and the desire to stay with Mei.

This expansion makes the “Song for me / Song for you / Song for all” chant feel different. When Mei awkwardly says “Song for me,” it is not just a ritual. It is a thesis:

> The girl who believed her face and personality made her unfit for school idols is now allowed to begin with herself.

That is the Season 2 version of Kanon’s original recovery.

# Updated Season 2 thesis

Episodes 1–2 established:

> **A dream that succeeds must learn how to remain reachable.**

Episodes 3–4 complicate that:

> **Remaining reachable does not mean lowering the dream. It means protecting the path by which frightened, awkward, ordinary, or self-denying people can choose to climb toward it.**

Wien represents greatness as selection: only the chosen, only the exceptional, only the ones with overwhelming desire.

Liella! represents greatness as connection: people who think they are not chosen can still become part of the song if they are willing to begin together.

That does not make Wien wrong about skill. Liella! lost. Her talent is real. But the philosophical opposition is now clear:

> **Wien asks who deserves to be chosen.
> Liella! asks what people can become when they are connected.**

That is the strongest development of these two episodes, and it gives Season 2 a much sharper conflict than simple member recruitment.

**Assistant — Turn 40**

Episodes 5–6 are the **Natsumi Onitsuka integration arc**, but more importantly they make Season 2’s central problem much sharper.

Up to this point, Season 2 has been asking:

> **How does Liella!, a group born from vulnerable beginnings, remain reachable after becoming admired and intimidating?**

Episodes 5–6 add a second problem:

> **What happens when Liella!’s sincerity enters the world of attention, views, monetization, editing, and performance-as-content?**

Natsumi is not just “the money girl.” She introduces a whole different value system into *Superstar!!*: not music as connection, not school idols as dreams, not performance as self-recovery, but performance as a route to survival, independence, and profit. The clever part is that the show first presents this as a gag, then slowly reveals it as a defense mechanism.

# Episode 5: Natsumi turns Liella! into content

Episode 5 begins by following up on Mei and Shiki joining. Liella! is now eight members, and Kinako is overjoyed that she finally has other first-years in the club. But the new first-year trio immediately confronts the gap between themselves and the second-years.

This is one of the episode’s most important early moves. Mei and Shiki are not weak in a generic sense. In fact, both have surprising ability: Mei has music sense, Shiki has physical aptitude, and both have knowledge acquired through private school-idol obsession. But when they compare themselves to the original five, they feel impossibly behind.

That means Kinako’s anxiety was not solved by adding more first-years. It has become a generational problem:

> The first-years admire Liella!, but admiration keeps turning into self-comparison.

That is exactly the vulnerability Natsumi exploits.

## Natsumi’s entrance: “producer,” not member

Natsumi does not come to Liella! as a fan, a dreamer, or a shy aspiring idol. She comes as a **producer**. That distinction matters.

Everyone else joined because school idols touched some hidden or wounded part of them. Natsumi approaches from the outside as an operator. She has seen Liella!’s follower count and views, and she immediately calculates opportunity. Her pitch is modern and plausible: she offers livestreaming support, promotion, online reach, editing, and branding. In other words, she understands the infrastructure of attention.

This makes her a useful foil for Sumire. Sumire also understands show business, but Sumire’s wound is theatrical recognition: she wants to be chosen, centered, and seen. Natsumi’s interest is colder and more transactional. She wants to convert visibility into money.

Her contract is telling: she charges no fee but collects all video revenue as “production costs.” Sumire notices the exploitative structure immediately, because Sumire has showbiz instincts. Keke and Kanon are more naïve because they are not thinking economically. Kanon even says they are not trying to make money, which is true, but also precisely why Natsumi can take advantage.

This is an unusually sharp little moment for *Love Live*: sincerity is admirable, but sincerity can be exploited by someone who understands contracts, platforms, and monetization.

## “Liella!’s everyday life” as demystification

The first video Natsumi films is about Liella!’s everyday life, and despite Natsumi’s motive, it has a real positive effect.

The first-years see the seniors playing cards, bickering, losing games, acting silly, discovering video games, and generally being normal teenagers. This directly addresses the Season 2 intimidation problem. The seniors had become mythic from the outside: Kanon the incredible singer, Chisato the dance champion, Ren the elegant student council president, Sumire the showbiz veteran, Keke the idol fanatic. But in ordinary life they are approachable, flawed, and ridiculous.

So Natsumi’s content is exploitative and useful at the same time.

That is important. The show does not make her wrong about everything. She understands something Liella! does not: public image is not only built from polished performances. Fans and prospective members also need to see ordinary personality, vulnerability, and play.

The video helps the first-years think:

> Maybe the seniors are not unreachable gods.
> Maybe they are people we can stand beside.

That is the good version of Natsumi’s worldview. Media can demystify. It can make a dream feel closer.

## Practice footage: the danger of comparison

The practice-video plan is more dangerous. Once Natsumi starts filming practice, the first-years become afraid that viewers will see the gap between them and the second-years. Their fear is not irrational. Liella! is now a public-facing group; footage is not neutral. A practice video can encourage fans, but it can also turn development into a spectacle and expose weakness before the members are ready.

This is a subtle but important distinction:

-   The everyday-life video makes the seniors less intimidating.

-   The practice video threatens to make the first-years feel inferior in public.


That is why Natsumi’s camera is not just a gag prop. It changes the social meaning of practice. Practice is supposed to be the private space where inadequacy can become growth. Natsumi turns it into content before the first-years have emotionally integrated their own beginner status.

## Kanon’s decision to wait

When Sumire exposes that Natsumi is using Liella! for video revenue, Kanon’s response is consistent with her established leadership style: she says they should wait and see because there is probably a reason.

Sumire is not wrong. In fact, Sumire is morally sharper here than Kanon. Natsumi is exploiting them. The contract is bad. The money motive is hidden. Sumire’s suspicion is justified.

But Kanon is also doing what Kanon always does: she looks for the wound beneath the behavior.

This is both her strength and her weakness. Her compassion lets people enter Liella! without being judged solely by their worst presentation. But it also risks enabling someone who is actively manipulating the group.

Sumire even says Kanon is too nice for her own good. She is right. Kanon’s instinct is beautiful, but not always safe.

This gives the episode a useful internal contrast:

> Sumire sees the scam.
> Kanon sees the possible reason behind the scam.
> Both perspectives are necessary.

That is why Liella! has become healthier as a group. Kanon alone might forgive too easily. Sumire alone might condemn too quickly. Together, they create a more complete moral response.

## Natsumi weaponizes the first-years’ insecurity

The most important turn in Episode 5 is Natsumi persuading the first-years to practice separately.

She frames it in a way that flatters their insecurity: if they train alone, they can avoid dragging down the second-years. This is exactly the wrong solution from Liella!’s perspective, but it feels emotionally plausible to Kinako, Mei, and Shiki. They already fear being exposed as weaker. Natsumi gives that fear a plan.

This is the darker mirror of Season 2’s earlier debate over accessibility. The first-years do not want an easier Liella!. They want to become useful to Liella!. But because they define usefulness through comparison, they become vulnerable to separation.

Chisato allows it because she understands the feeling. She once believed she had to become strong alone before standing beside Kanon. That is a lovely continuity point. She knows this is not ideal, but she also respects that the first-years may need to test themselves.

Episode 5 ends with Natsumi triumphant: she has split them up and can “play” with them. But the viewer already understands the tragic irony. Natsumi thinks she is manipulating naïve girls for money. What she is actually doing is walking into the exact emotional world that will expose her own lack of a dream.

# Episode 6: Natsumi meets dreamers

Episode 6 moves the first-years and Natsumi to Hokkaido, Kinako’s home territory. This is a smart location shift because Hokkaido gives the first-years a physical space separate from the seniors’ aura. The visual openness — fields, sky, rural roads, Kinako’s family homestay — contrasts with Yuigaoka’s prestige and Tokyo intensity. It lets the first-years temporarily ask who they are without standing in the original five’s shadow.

But Natsumi is still trying to frame everything as content.

She wants a unit name, a sister-group structure, daily-life footage, clickbait, and eventually manipulative titles like “Liella!’s Disbandment.” The comedy is broad, but the underlying logic is sharp: Natsumi thinks narrative conflict, separation, and insecurity are useful because they generate attention.

In her worldview, even emotional pain can become material.

That makes her one of the more modern characters in *Love Live*. She is not merely a showbiz girl like Sumire. She belongs to the platform economy: thumbnails, branding, subscriptions, superchats, engagement, clickbait, and monetized personal image.

## The first-years reject becoming a “sister group”

The first scene is important because Kinako, Mei, and Shiki reject Natsumi’s attempt to turn them into a separate unit. They do not want to become Liella!’s sister group. They want to become useful to Liella!.

That might sound unhealthy — and to a degree it is — but it also clarifies their sincerity. They are not trying to escape the seniors or create their own fame. They want to close the gap so the nine-member Liella! can win.

Mei’s line is especially severe: if she cannot be useful to Liella!, she has no intention of continuing as a school idol. That shows how quickly her hidden fandom has turned into responsibility. She does not merely want to enjoy being close to idols. She wants to be worthy of standing with them.

So the first-years’ problem is not lack of seriousness. It is over-identification with usefulness.

This connects them to earlier arcs:

-   Chisato once tied belonging to usefulness.

-   Sumire tied visibility to being chosen.

-   Kanon tied singing to not disappointing others.

-   The first-years now tie membership to helping Liella! win.


The same emotional pattern keeps reappearing in new forms.

## Natsumi’s dreamlessness

The key to Natsumi is that she does not initially understand dreams as something worth trusting. When Kinako asks about her dream, Natsumi says she has none — unless earning money counts. When asked why money, her answer is comically practical: retirement security.

That joke is actually revealing. Natsumi’s worldview is built around future anxiety. Money is not just greed. It is control. It is the one measurable thing she can pursue after other dreams have failed.

Later, she says it explicitly: she has had many dreams, and none came true. So she concluded she is not the kind of dreamer the others are.

That is the emotional center of the arc.

Natsumi’s “money makes the world go round” persona is not pure avarice. It is a defensive philosophy built from failed aspiration:

> Dreams disappoint.
> Money is real.
> Views are measurable.
> Profit is proof.
> Therefore, chase money, not dreams.

That makes her much more interesting than a simple comic scammer.

## Kinako and Natsumi

Kinako is the perfect character to expose Natsumi because Kinako’s sincerity is almost impossible to deflect. She is not clever enough to outmaneuver Natsumi and not suspicious enough to condemn her. She simply asks: what is your dream?

That question pierces Natsumi more than an accusation would.

Kinako’s own dream is also important: she wants to surpass the senior performance and make Liella! even better than before. This is ambitious, maybe naïve, but it is not selfish. It is the dream of a kouhai who wants to add to what she inherited.

Natsumi first mocks or resists that kind of idealism, but when she films the first-years practicing, she sees that their dream is not empty. It is difficult, but not impossible. Her line that if Kinako is just going to give up, she should not talk about having a dream is harsh, but it is also the first time Natsumi speaks from genuine investment rather than profit motive.

This is her turning point before she realizes it.

She is moved by the sight of a possible dream.

## Chisato raises the stakes

Back in Tokyo, Chisato sees the first-years’ video and decides it is time for them to learn the same choreography as the second-years. This is a major Season 2 development.

Previously, Liella! had protected the first-years by reducing pressure. Then the first-years chose the serious path. Now Chisato gives them a concrete standard: same steps, same stage, same group.

This is the right evolution. Chisato is not throwing them into difficulty randomly. She has observed their growth and gives them a goal.

Her line about planning being easier when there is a goal fits the whole season. The first-years need an endpoint that is hard enough to organize their effort. Otherwise “catching up” remains a vague anxiety.

So Episode 6 refines Season 2’s accessibility thesis again:

> A reachable dream still needs a demanding form.
> Without a standard, encouragement becomes fog.

## Kanon’s intervention

Kanon arriving in Hokkaido in a bear suit is comic, but the scene that follows is one of the key moments of Season 2. She has come because she is worried about both the first-years and Natsumi. This is exactly Kanon’s leadership style: she senses the emotional loose thread and physically goes to where the problem is.

Her invitation to Natsumi is also very Kanon:

> If you do not have a dream, why not chase the same one as everyone else?

This is a profound contrast with Natsumi’s worldview. Natsumi thinks dreams are individual bets that fail and leave you alone. Kanon offers a communal dream — not as a replacement for personal ambition, but as a way to start moving before you know what your own dream is.

Kanon then uses her own history again. She says she failed to enter Yuigaoka’s music course, but Keke and the others taught her that if people are together, they can do their best. If they put together their faults and shortcomings, they can chase dreams together.

This is the most explicit statement of Kanon’s mature philosophy so far:

> **A group does not exist because every member is already whole.
> A group exists because incomplete people can combine their shortcomings into forward motion.**

That is the exact opposite of Wien’s “chosen talent” philosophy and the exact opposite of Natsumi’s “money is the only reliable thing” philosophy.

## Natsumi joins Liella!

The actual joining is not framed as Natsumi suddenly becoming pure. She remains embarrassed, uncertain, and still tied to her streaming persona. But the school festival performance makes her realize she has found something.

The staging is important. The nine-member Liella! enters as “a new Liella!” and counts from one to nine. This directly pays off the earlier “nine is the magic number” joke, but also makes the number emotionally real. It is no longer Keke projecting franchise mythology onto the group. It is Liella! actually becoming a nine-member organism.

Natsumi’s presence changes the performance atmosphere. The song and visuals are bright, flashy, almost neon-commercial in a way that fits her. It is not just “Liella! plus Natsumi.” It is Liella! absorbing her content-production, extroverted, attention-grabbing energy and turning it into school-idol expression.

That matters because the show does not reject Natsumi’s media persona. It redeems it.

Her camera, streaming language, self-promotion, and “OniNat-’sup!” energy are not erased. They are repositioned from exploitation toward participation. She becomes the one who can open the performance, frame the event, and bring viewers into the spectacle — but now as a member rather than an extractor.

At the end, she says she found her dream. That lands because the dream is not simply “become a school idol” in the abstract. It is:

> To stand inside the kind of collective dream she had stopped believing could belong to her.

# Natsumi Onitsuka: running profile

Natsumi is one of the most conceptually useful Season 2 additions because she introduces a value system the franchise usually treats only lightly.

Her surface personality:

-   loud

-   opportunistic

-   shamelessly self-promotional

-   money-obsessed

-   media-savvy

-   theatrical

-   evasive when confronted

-   surprisingly hard-working


Her deeper structure:

> **Natsumi is a failed dreamer who replaced aspiration with monetization.**

She has tried many dreams, failed at them, and concluded that she is not “the kind of dreamer” the others are. Money becomes her substitute for a dream because money is concrete. It does not require vulnerability in the same way.

This is why she is not the same type as Sumire. Sumire still believes in stardom even after repeated rejection; her arrogance is the armor of someone who cannot stop wanting the spotlight. Natsumi’s armor is more cynical. She has already retreated from dreaming into calculation.

Sumire says, “I should be seen.”
Natsumi says, “Visibility should pay.”

That difference is important.

## Natsumi’s core wound

Her wound is not poverty in a simple melodramatic sense, though she clearly works hard and worries about money. Her wound is repeated dream failure. She has internalized the idea that dreaming belongs to other people.

So her “money” mantra is not merely greed. It is anti-disappointment technology.

If you chase money, then every view, every like, every metric, every revenue estimate gives feedback. If you chase a dream, you risk discovering that your heart was foolish.

That is what Kanon has to counter.

## Natsumi’s relationship to Liella!

Natsumi initially sees Liella! as an asset. By the end of Episode 6, she sees Liella! as a place where she might become a person with a dream again.

That is the conversion.

But I would not say her arc is complete. She has found the dream, but she has not yet fully integrated her media/commercial instincts into Liella!’s ethical framework. That will probably remain her ongoing role: keeping Liella! visible and contemporary while needing reminders that attention is not the same as connection.

# Kanon Shibuya: updated profile through S2E6

Kanon’s leadership reaches one of its clearest forms here.

In Season 1, Kanon was often the person being saved. Then she became the person who could reinterpret other people’s blocked dreams. In Season 2, she increasingly becomes the person who can invite someone into a dream before that person has a dream of her own.

That is a subtle but important distinction.

Natsumi does not say, “I secretly want to be a school idol.”
She says, “I do not have a dream.”

Kanon’s answer is not, “You must have one deep down.” It is:

> Then borrow ours. Start with us.

This is mature because Kanon understands from experience that desire can be reawakened by participation. She did not recover her own voice in isolation. Keke’s belief came first; the feeling followed. She now offers Natsumi the same structure: enter the shared movement, and perhaps your own desire will appear.

That is exactly what happens.

## Kanon’s compassion and its risks

Episode 5 also makes Kanon’s softness look risky. Sumire is right that Natsumi is exploiting them. Kanon’s “there must be a reason” is emotionally accurate but practically dangerous.

This is a good limitation for her. Kanon’s strongest trait — seeing the wounded self beneath bad behavior — can become naïveté if not balanced by others. She needs Sumire’s suspicion, Chisato’s structure, Ren’s institutional seriousness, and Keke’s intensity.

Kanon is not a flawless moral center. She is an interpretive center. That distinction matters.

## Kanon as the anti-Wien

The contrast with Wien keeps sharpening even though Wien is absent from these episodes.

Wien’s implied worldview: only exceptional individuals deserve the stage.
Natsumi’s initial worldview: dreams are unreliable; monetize attention instead.
Kanon’s worldview: flawed people can chase dreams together.

These are three very different responses to disappointment:

-   Wien intensifies talent and selection.

-   Natsumi retreats into money and metrics.

-   Kanon creates shared beginnings.


That is becoming the philosophical spine of Season 2.

# Chisato: updated profile

Chisato’s presidency continues to matter. In Episode 5, she adjusts training for the first-years; in Episode 6, she decides to raise the stakes once she sees they are ready.

This shows that Chisato is becoming a genuinely good club president. She is not simply gentle. She knows when to protect and when to push. Her Season 1 arc prepared her for this: because she once misunderstood strength as solitary proof, she understands the first-years’ desire to train separately. But because she now understands mutual support, she also knows that they eventually need to stand on the same level as the others.

Her leadership is bodily and developmental:

> Kanon invites emotionally.
> Chisato calibrates the path of training.

That division of labor is increasingly clear.

# Sumire: updated profile

Sumire is excellent in Episode 5 because she is the first to recognize Natsumi’s exploitation. This is not just because she is cynical. It is because she understands show business and money better than most of Liella!.

Her suspicion protects the group.

This continues her Season 2 role as the practical counterweight to Kanon’s generosity. Sumire may be vain and abrasive, but she has worldly instincts the others often lack. She knows that contracts matter, revenue matters, and hidden terms matter. In a story about school idols entering the media economy, Sumire is one of the few characters equipped to smell danger.

# The first-years after Episodes 5–6

Kinako, Mei, and Shiki become more coherent as a sub-unit here, even though they reject the idea of being a separate sister group.

Their shared identity is:

> first-years who want to become useful to Liella! without being carried by the seniors.

Kinako gives sincerity and the dream language.
Mei gives pride, music sensitivity, and fan seriousness.
Shiki gives competence, tools, and analytic framing.

What they lack is confidence that their developing selves are already part of Liella!, not future applicants to Liella!. That is why the school festival matters. The performance forces them onto the same stage, same choreography, same count-off, same name.

They do not become Liella! after they catch up.

They catch up by being Liella!.

# Liella! after Episodes 5–6

With Natsumi joining, the group finally becomes nine. But this matters less as a franchise-number callback than as a structural turning point.

Liella! now contains three different generations of entry into the dream:

The original five: recovered blocked selves through school idols.
Kinako/Mei/Shiki: intimidated first-years learning to enter an already-bright group.
Natsumi: a cynical, media-savvy failed dreamer rediscovering desire through the group.

This makes Liella! much broader than Season 1’s unit. Season 1 was about forming a group from emotional wounds. Season 2 is about turning that group into a culture that can absorb different relationships to aspiration:

-   pure admiration

-   hidden fandom

-   loneliness

-   usefulness anxiety

-   cynicism

-   commercialism

-   failed dreams


That is a big expansion.

# Updated Season 2 thesis

After Episodes 5–6, I would refine the Season 2 thesis again:

> **A successful dream must remain reachable, but it must also survive being seen, marketed, compared, and monetized.**

Natsumi reveals that public visibility is double-edged. Liella!’s videos can inspire people, demystify the seniors, and give the first-years confidence. But they can also expose weakness, create comparison, incentivize clickbait, and convert sincerity into revenue.

The answer is not to reject media. Liella! needs media. Natsumi’s skills matter. The answer is to place media inside the group’s ethic of connection.

That is what Natsumi’s joining accomplishes.

She does not stop being OniNatsu.
She stops standing outside the dream with a camera and starts standing inside it with everyone else.

The strongest statement of these episodes is therefore:

> **Attention becomes dangerous when it extracts from people.
> It becomes beautiful when it helps people join the song.**

**Assistant — Turn 42**

Episodes 7–8 are quieter than the Natsumi arc, but they are structurally important because they refine Season 2’s central question. Season 2 has been about how Liella! can grow from a five-member miracle into a nine-member school culture. These episodes ask:

> **Can the people who carry Yuigaoka and Liella! allow themselves to be ordinary, vulnerable, and supported by others?**

Episode 7 does that through Ren. Episode 8 does it through Yuigaoka as a whole.

The key movement is:

> Ren stops treating herself as the solitary guardian of her mother’s school.
> Yuigaoka becomes a school where responsibilities, dreams, and stages are shared.

# Episode 7: Ren Hazuki and the shame of being ordinary

Episode 7 initially looks like a comic “Ren becomes addicted to video games” episode, but it is actually a continuation of Ren’s Season 1 wound. Ren’s central problem has always been that she treats Yuigaoka as something she must guard alone. In Season 1, that made her oppose the school idol club because she misunderstood her mother’s legacy. In Season 2, that same seriousness makes her overwork herself as student council president.

The Love Live prelims are announced as fully remote, with free song choice. Chisato suggests the familiar production structure: Kanon writes lyrics, Ren composes. But Ren is exhausted, distracted, and half-asleep. Everyone assumes she is overworked by the student council, which is partly true. But the actual source of her immediate problem is more embarrassing: she has discovered games.

That embarrassment is the point.

Ren can confess grief.
Ren can confess institutional anxiety.
Ren can confess devotion to her mother’s school.
But she cannot confess something as ordinary as “I got hooked on games and stayed up too late.”

That tells us a lot about her self-image.

## Ren’s perfection problem

Ren is not merely responsible. She believes that her responsibility requires an image of near-flawlessness. She says she is the student council president, the person holding the school together. If anyone finds out that she is addicted to games, she imagines disaster: disappointment, loss of authority, even damage to the school idol club.

That is absurd in the literal sense, but emotionally coherent for Ren. She has spent so long being “Hazuki Ren, founder’s daughter, student council president, guardian of Yuigaoka” that ordinary weakness feels like institutional betrayal.

This is why the comedy works. The game addiction is silly, but Ren’s shame is real.

Her fear is not:

> “People will think I like games.”

It is:

> “People will realize I am not the perfect person I think I must be.”

That makes Episode 7 a very natural sequel to her Season 1 arc. She no longer misreads her mother’s dream, but she still misreads what it means to inherit that dream. She thinks inheritance requires solitary dignity. The episode teaches her that inheritance can also mean ordinary happiness with friends.

## Mei as Ren’s unlikely confidante

The choice to have Mei discover Ren’s secret is surprisingly good. Mei understands secret desire. Her whole entry into Liella! was built around being unable to admit that she loved school idols. So when she finds Ren hiding a hobby, she is the right person to understand the shame of wanting something that does not fit one’s self-image.

Mei’s advice to Ren is also emotionally mature. She tells Ren that friends may fight when they learn more about each other, but that is part of growing closer. This directly reflects Mei’s own relationship with Shiki. She and Shiki became closer not because they hid their embarrassing desires, but because they finally exposed them.

So Mei becomes a bridge from the first-year arc into Ren’s arc:

> Mei learned that hidden desire can be accepted.
> Now she helps Ren learn the same thing.

This is one of the better signs that the new members are not just add-ons. They are starting to affect the seniors.

## Kanon as vice president

Kanon’s decision to ask Ren to make her student council vice president is another major leadership moment. It is not glamorous. It is not musical. But it is extremely important.

Kanon sees that Ren is carrying too much, and she does not merely offer emotional support. She offers institutional labor. She wants to help Ren grow the school inherited from Ren’s mother. That line matters because Kanon understands what Yuigaoka means to Ren. She is not just saying, “Let me help with paperwork.” She is saying:

> I understand that this school is your mother’s dream, and I want to help you carry it.

This is a mature evolution of Kanon’s relationship with Ren. In Season 1, Kanon helped Ren reinterpret her mother’s past. In Season 2, Kanon helps Ren bear her mother’s future.

There is also a subtle difference between Kanon and Chisato here. Chisato became school idol club president to support Liella!’s internal organization. Kanon becomes student council vice president to support Yuigaoka as a whole. This expands Kanon’s sphere from group interpreter to school-level participant.

## The misunderstanding comedy

The “forbidden realm” misunderstanding around Ren and Mei is funny, but it also serves a function. It shows that Liella! has become intimate enough to meddle, tease, speculate, and panic about each other’s lives. This is not the solemn Season 1 group formed under existential emotional wounds. It is now a messy, ordinary friend group.

That matters for Ren. She does not need to be loved only as the dignified president. She can be teased, misunderstood, and embarrassed. That is part of ordinary closeness.

## Multiplayer as the episode’s metaphor

The final gaming scene is the emotional solution in miniature. Ren has been stuck on a boss because it is too hard for a solo player. The others join in multiplayer. Keke supports, Natsumi attacks, Kanon turns around when warned, everyone contributes, and together they clear it.

This is not subtle, but it is exactly right for the episode.

Ren’s life has been a solo-player challenge.
The answer is multiplayer.

That metaphor is especially fitting because it translates the school idol ethic into Ren’s new hobby. The lesson is not “stop playing games and return to perfect responsibility.” The lesson is:

> Even the thing you were ashamed of can become a place of connection.

That is a beautiful little resolution. Ren does not have to lock the gaming room and renounce her ordinary interests. She can keep the key. She can enjoy games without letting them consume her. She can be responsible and still be human.

Her final reflection — that something so simple can change how she feels, and that her mother’s school has led her to so many wonderful encounters — is the true endpoint. Yuigaoka is no longer a burden she protects alone. It is the place that gave her friends who can see her imperfectly and still stay.

# Ren Hazuki: updated profile through S2E7

Ren’s profile is now much fuller.

Her original wound:

> She inherited her mother’s dream but misread its meaning.

Her Season 2 wound:

> She still believes she must be worthy of that inheritance by remaining composed, responsible, and flawless.

Episode 7 does not undermine her seriousness. It humanizes it. Ren is still elegant, disciplined, dutiful, and deeply attached to Yuigaoka. But now she is also someone who can get hooked on games, lose sleep, panic about being found out, and need help from her friends.

This is important because Ren’s perfection was always isolating. In Season 1, it separated her from Liella!. In Season 2, it threatens to separate her from ordinary friendship.

Her growth is:

> From solitary guardian
> to shared steward
> to ordinary friend.

That is a strong continuation.

# Episode 8: finding the stage where roads meet

Episode 8 is the district prelim stage episode, and it works as a companion to Episode 12 of Season 1. In Season 1, Yuigaoka’s students built Liella!’s Christmas stage. In Season 2, the question becomes: what stage represents the nine-member Liella! and Yuigaoka now?

The answer is no longer merely “the school supporting Liella!.” It becomes:

> **Yuigaoka as a place where roads, people, dreams, and futures cross.**

This is a very *Superstar!!* idea, and it is one of the cleanest uses of location in the series so far.

## The remote prelim problem

Because the prelims are remote and song choice is unrestricted, stage choice becomes strategically crucial. Keke correctly points out that historical Love Live stages have mattered. Natsumi immediately thinks in terms of appeal, polling, and viewer incentives. Sumire and the others understand that they need impact. But Kanon frames the problem most clearly:

> They need a place that symbolizes Liella!, Yuigaoka, and each of them.

That is a harder standard than “a famous place” or “a flashy place.” It requires interpretation.

The rejected Jingu Stadium idea is funny because it is impossible, but it also shows the temptation to borrow grandeur. A huge venue would make them look important. But it would not necessarily say anything about Liella!.

Natsumi’s vote-for-exclusive-pictures proposal is the opposite temptation: not grandeur, but attention manipulation. Again, the show is keeping her media logic alive. She thinks in terms of incentives and votes. That is useful in moderation, but the episode rejects it as the group’s core strategy.

Liella!’s real answer has to come from meaning, not spectacle or manipulation.

## The open house and Yuigaoka’s maturation

The open house scenes are more than school-event comedy. They show that Yuigaoka has changed.

A magazine describes the school as small but united. Prospective students visit. Clubs are active. The student council is no longer Ren alone. Kinako and Nanami are involved, and Kanon is serving alongside Ren. Students from different courses work together. The school is becoming what Ren’s mother wanted.

That line — “We’ve become the kind of school your mom wished for” — is a quiet but major payoff to Season 1. Yuigaoka’s founding ideal is no longer only a remembered wish in Ren’s family archive. It is becoming visible in everyday school life.

This is also why the school idol club chooses not to hold an open house concert. They have enough status now that a concert would dominate attention and overshadow the other clubs. That is a mature institutional choice.

In Season 1, Liella! needed the school to notice them.
In Season 2, Liella! must avoid swallowing the school’s identity.

That is an important development. The group has become powerful enough that restraint matters.

## Aria’s visit

Aria’s visit is a small but interesting Kanon-family moment. Keke immediately tries to recruit her for next year, and the “both sisters as school idols” joke is obvious. But Aria’s actual contribution is different: she asks why someone decided to build a school there in the first place.

That question unlocks the episode’s interpretive path.

Aria is an outsider-adjacent figure: close enough to Kanon to understand her, but not inside Yuigaoka’s daily emotional economy. Because she has not absorbed the school’s assumptions, she can ask the basic historical question everyone else takes for granted.

This is a nice use of her. She does not need to become a school idol to matter to the story. She helps Kanon think.

## Sunny Passion’s last island stage

Sunny Passion’s video call is one of the episode’s strongest thematic parallels. They show Liella! their stage, built by everyone on the island to fit their image. They explain that this may be the last time they can perform on that island as school idols, because later rounds will take place in Tokyo or at the national venue.

This gives Sunny Passion a gentle melancholy. Their island stage is not just pretty. It is a farewell to performing as local representatives in the place that made them.

Sunny Passion’s relationship to place has always been clearer than Liella!’s. They are island school idols. Their school, island, audience, and stage are organically aligned. They are proud of that rootedness.

Liella! sees this and realizes they need their own equivalent:

> not a borrowed grand venue,
> not a vote-hacking gimmick,
> but a stage that expresses where their feelings gather.

Sunny Passion once again functions as the mature foil. They show Liella! what it looks like when place and performance are unified.

## Kanon and Kinako walking the town

Kanon’s walk with Kinako is the emotional hinge of the episode. Kinako, as the girl from Hokkaido, sees Tokyo/Yuigaoka’s surroundings with fresh eyes: lively streets, fashionable people, filming, smiles, crowds, roads, motion. She is excited because the city still feels new to her.

Kanon, who grew up there, has to learn to see her familiar world again through Kinako’s outsider wonder.

That is crucial. Kanon finds the stage by combining memory and freshness:

-   Kanon knows the area intimately.

-   Kinako sees its liveliness as something special.

-   Aria has asked why the school was built there.

-   Sunny Passion has reminded them that place matters.

-   The student council has gathered stage suggestions from the school.

-   The old roads have existed for over a hundred years.


All of this converges in Kanon’s insight: Yuigaoka was built where roads come together.

This is one of Kanon’s most characteristic interpretive moments. She gathers fragments from other people and turns them into a unifying image.

## The road as stage

Choosing the road is a very strong symbolic choice. Roads are movement, crossing, arrival, departure, encounter. They are not owned by one person. They exist because people pass through them.

That makes the road a perfect stage for Season 2 Liella!.

Season 1 Liella! was born from five girls whose lives converged at Yuigaoka. Season 2 Liella! now includes first-years, the student council, prospective students, the town, and a school whose identity is still forming. The road expresses all of that.

Kanon’s explanation makes it explicit:

> The roads come together here.
> All of us come together here.
> Our dreams and hopes connect here.
> This is what Yuigaoka is about.

That is the episode’s thesis.

The road stage also develops the “Song for me / Song for you / Song for all” chant. Season 1 moved from personal recovery to school connection. Season 2’s road stage expands that connection outward into the town, open house visitors, future students, and those who may cross paths with Yuigaoka.

The performance invitation — “We hope that Liella! and Yuigaoka will cross paths with you” — is exactly right. It is not just “watch us.” It is “come into relation with us.”

# Kanon Shibuya: updated profile through S2E8

Kanon’s Season 2 leadership keeps becoming more institutional and spatial.

In Season 1, she interpreted people.
In Season 2, she increasingly interprets places, roles, and structures.

Episode 7: she notices Ren’s burden and enters the student council.
Episode 8: she interprets Yuigaoka’s location and turns it into Liella!’s stage.

That is a real expansion of her protagonist function. She is no longer only the emotional center of Liella!. She is becoming one of Yuigaoka’s meaning-makers.

## Kanon as vice president

Kanon becoming vice president is more important than it first appears. It formalizes her responsibility to the school, not merely the club. This creates a bridge between Liella! and Yuigaoka’s broader student body.

This also corrects a possible imbalance from Season 1. Kanon’s singing and emotional force brought people together, but Ren carried the school institutionally. Now Kanon helps carry that institutional load. She is not replacing Ren; she is sharing the burden.

That is a mature expression of friendship: not only comforting Ren, but taking on work.

## Kanon as interpreter of place

The road-stage insight is classic Kanon. She does not invent meaning alone. She absorbs what everyone else reveals:

-   Kinako’s outsider excitement.

-   Aria’s question.

-   Sunny Passion’s rootedness.

-   Ren’s mother’s dream.

-   the students’ desire to support Liella!.

-   the history of Omotesando’s roads.


Then she names the connection.

This is the same gift she used when naming Liella!. She takes apparent incoherence — roads, school history, open house, stage ideas, town energy — and turns it into a song/stage concept.

Her creative talent is therefore not only musical. It is synthetic. She finds the shared image that lets everyone move together.

# Ren and Kanon as school stewards

Episodes 7–8 make Ren and Kanon into a complementary pair at the institutional level.

Ren carries memory.
Kanon creates beginnings.

Ren guards Yuigaoka’s inheritance.
Kanon helps that inheritance become livable and shared.

Ren understands the school as her mother’s dream.
Kanon helps connect that dream to the students now present.

This is why Kanon as vice president makes emotional sense. Ren alone risks turning school stewardship into duty. Kanon brings relational warmth and forward motion. Together, they make Yuigaoka less like a memorial and more like a living school.

# Chisato and distributed responsibility

Chisato also quietly benefits from this arc. In Episode 7, she tells Kanon that she challenged herself to become club president, repeating Kanon’s own earlier lesson back to her. In Episode 8, she is part of the emotional response when the students offer to take over student council work so Liella! can focus on Love Live.

This matters because Season 2 has become a story of distributed responsibility:

-   Chisato handles the school idol club.

-   Ren leads the student council.

-   Kanon supports Ren as vice president.

-   Kinako and Nanami enter the council.

-   students help with stage ideas and school work.

-   Liella! carries Yuigaoka into Love Live.

-   Yuigaoka carries Liella! by giving them the stage.


No single person has to hold everything.

This is the direct opposite of early Ren, and also a correction to early Kanon’s tendency to internalize pressure.

# Kinako’s role in Episode 8

Kinako is very important in this episode even though she is not the central emotional focus. Her outsider perspective helps Kanon rediscover her own town. That fits Kinako’s Season 2 role as the permeability figure: she is both inside Liella! and still new enough to see things freshly.

She also symbolizes Yuigaoka becoming a place that people come to from elsewhere. Season 1’s Yuigaoka was Kanon and Ren’s local emotional world. Season 2’s Yuigaoka now includes girls from Hokkaido, Shanghai, and other backgrounds, plus prospective students looking in from outside.

Kinako’s wonder at the city helps Liella! understand that the road stage is not ordinary just because it is familiar to them.

# Natsumi after joining

Natsumi remains wonderfully herself. Her instinct is still to think in terms of virality, breaking exclusive footage, and monetizable attention. But now she is inside the group’s ethical frame. When Sunny Passion shows their stage, Natsumi’s first instinct is to make it viral, but the others stop her because Sunny Passion trusts them.

That is exactly where her ongoing development should be. She still has the media reflex. The group teaches her when not to exploit trust.

This is more interesting than simply purifying her. Natsumi’s instincts remain useful but need moral calibration.

# Liella! after Episodes 7–8

By the end of Episode 8, Liella! is now fully functioning as a nine-member group embedded in a school.

That sounds simple, but it took a lot of structural work:

-   The first-years have joined.

-   Natsumi has joined.

-   Chisato is club president.

-   Kanon is student council vice president.

-   Ren is less isolated.

-   The student council includes younger/general-course students.

-   The school itself supports Liella! without being swallowed by them.

-   The district prelim stage expresses Yuigaoka, not just the idols.


This is very different from Season 1. Season 1 was a formation story. Season 2 is becoming a civic/community story: what happens when the idol group becomes one of the institutions through which a school understands itself?

The remote prelim performance is therefore not simply a competition entry. It is Yuigaoka staging its identity to the outside world.

# Updated Season 2 thesis

After Episodes 7–8, I would refine the Season 2 thesis again:

> **A dream that succeeds must remain reachable, but it also must become shareable as responsibility, not merely admiration.**

Liella! becoming popular was not enough.
Adding first-years was not enough.
Becoming nine members was not enough.
They also had to distribute work, expose imperfections, and find a stage that belonged to everyone.

Episode 7 teaches that even Ren can be ordinary and supported.
Episode 8 teaches that even Liella!’s stage is not theirs alone; it belongs to the school, the town, the roads, and the people whose paths cross there.

That is the deepest continuity with Season 1. Kanon began as a girl whose voice failed when she stood alone. By Season 2 Episode 8, the whole school is built around the opposite truth:

> **No one’s song, dream, school, or future is meant to be carried alone.**
