# *Love Live! Superstar!!* — Season 3, Episode 6

## Audio-integrated analysis and cumulative character profiles

I reviewed the complete Season 3, Episode 6 analysis bundle: 301 Japanese subtitle cues, 962 retained frames, 49 contact sheets, and the complete episode audio through the final preview dialogue. As anticipated from the original manifest, the extracted MP3 ends about 2.5 seconds before the nominal video endpoint, but no narrative dialogue appears to be lost.

This is principally **Keke’s graduation-vocation episode**, but it is also a major culmination for Kanon, an unexpectedly important step for Wien, and a practical demonstration of how the temporary TomaKanoTe structure changes all three of its members.

Its central question is:

> **Does gratitude toward the people who allowed you to pursue a dream require knowing when to stop—or require becoming honest about how completely the dream has changed your life?**

Keke has convinced herself that her three years in Japan constitute a finished allowance:

-   her parents trusted her;

-   she was permitted to pursue school idols;

-   she experienced an extraordinary youth;

-   she should now repay that trust by returning to the safe educational route she would have taken without Liella!.


The episode reveals the hidden violence in that reasoning.

Keke is treating the discovery of her vocation as though it were a temporary indulgence. She speaks of school idols as something she was **allowed to do**, rather than something through which she discovered who she had become.

Kanon’s answer is especially powerful because it returns Keke’s own original voice to her:

> The words with which Keke once prevented Kanon from declaring singing “over” have become one of Kanon’s treasures.
> Kanon now refuses to let Keke declare her own love “over.”

The episode therefore completes one of the longest vocal-relational circles in *Superstar!!*:

> Keke once gave Kanon permission to continue.
> Kanon preserved those words inside herself.
> When Keke can no longer believe them, Kanon speaks them back.

---

# I. MoeMoe’s plan: make Keke’s future visible to her parents

Keke’s older sister explains why she has brought Kanon, Wien, and Tomari to the Shanghai school-idol festival:

> 「可可ちゃんを助けて」
> “Help Keke.”

Her plan is practical. Keke has been unable to speak honestly with their parents about continuing along the path of song. If the parents see her shining onstage, they may understand that this is not merely a youthful hobby.

MoeMoe says:

> 「可可ちゃんは、これからも歌の道を突き進むのです」
> “Keke will continue pushing forward along the path of song.”

Her delivery and syntax are strikingly certain. She speaks about Keke’s future with more confidence than Keke herself can presently manage.

That confidence is both loving and revealing. MoeMoe sees the continuity between:

-   the girl who loved school idols;

-   the student who crossed national borders;

-   the founder of Liella!;

-   and a future singer.


Keke has artificially divided those selves:

> school-idol Keke belongs to high school;
> responsible adult Keke belongs to university.

MoeMoe refuses the division.

Her use of performance as persuasion also fits the series’ wider philosophy. She does not believe a résumé or verbal argument will adequately communicate what school idols mean to Keke. The family must **see and hear the transformed person**.

---

# II. The three-member group initially refuses Liella!’s problem

Wien immediately insists:

> 「私たちには関係ないでしょ」
> “This has nothing to do with us.”

And:

> 「Liella!は敵よ」
> “Liella! is the enemy.”

Kanon points out that MoeMoe invited them too. Wien replies that the invitation resulted from not knowing they had become a separate group.

Tomari offers the superficially sensible conclusion:

> 「ここは素直に、観光を楽しむのがベストかと」
> “It would be best simply to enjoy sightseeing.”

Their three positions initially remain distinct:

-   **Kanon** hears that someone needs help.

-   **Wien** protects the rival boundary.

-   **Tomari** rejects unrequested emotional involvement as inefficient.


Yet the episode’s later structure transforms all three.

TomaKanoTe ultimately uses its own stage time not to defeat Liella!, advertise itself, or secure a measurable benefit, but to create the emotional conditions in which Keke can stand before her hometown as someone publicly wanted.

That is especially meaningful after Season 3, Episode 2. The group that formed to demonstrate the value of performance after measurable success became impossible now applies that lesson to another person’s future.

---

# III. Shanghai and Keke’s double life

The episode spends substantial time allowing Shanghai to function as more than an exotic backdrop.

For Keke, it is simultaneously:

-   home;

-   family obligation;

-   the place she left;

-   the audience before whom she must justify what leaving accomplished;

-   and the site where school-idol Keke and daughter Keke can no longer remain separated.


In Japan, Keke’s loud declaration of school-idol love created her social identity.

In Shanghai, the same identity must face the people whose generosity made it possible.

That changes her voice.

Her ordinary school-idol register is:

-   bright;

-   high-energy;

-   quick;

-   emotionally unembarrassed;

-   full of emphatic 「デス」 endings;

-   eager to declare love publicly.


When the future and her parents enter the conversation, the brightness narrows. She becomes calmer, more formally reasoned, and almost eerily resigned.

She is not struggling to explain what she wants.

She is explaining why what she wants must now end.

---

# IV. The eight-member Liella! chooses Keke as center

Because Kanon is practicing separately with Wien and Tomari, Liella!’s other eight members prepare the Shanghai performance without its original central singer.

This is already important for the larger inheritance structure of the series. Liella! can rehearse, plan, and distribute responsibility without Kanon physically occupying the center of every process.

The first-years propose Keke as center:

> 「私たちは、可可ちゃんに立ってもらいたい」
> “We want Keke-chan to stand at center.”

Mei supports it openly:

> 「私たちは大賛成だぜ」
> “We strongly agree.”

Shiki adds her characteristically compressed:

> 「Me too.」

The choice acknowledges several things:

-   Shanghai is Keke’s home.

-   Keke is one of Liella!’s founders.

-   Her school-idol devotion made the group possible.

-   Her juniors now regard her not merely as the excitable foreign member, but as a senior whose presence deserves to organize the stage.


Keke initially responds:

> 「可可が、センター？」
> “Keke, as center?”

The self-reference **可可** is characteristic of her speech, but here it also sounds unusually young. She is momentarily less the veteran school idol than the daughter surprised that the whole group has chosen her in her hometown.

When she accepts—

> 「是非やらせてクダサイ」
> “Please allow me to do it.”

—the enthusiasm is real. But it is already shadowed by the private conclusion that this will be one of her final stages.

The others hear a triumphant homecoming.

Keke hears a farewell performance.

---

# V. Kanon and Keke over tea: love is easier to confess than desire

Kanon meets Keke after practice.

Their conversation begins with striking directness:

> Keke tells Kanon she is always kind.
> Keke says: 「大好きデス」 — “I love you.”
> Kanon answers: 「私もだよ」 — “Me too.”

The exchange is neither played as a joke nor followed by panicked retraction. It reflects the unusual openness at the foundation of their relationship.

Keke can say that she loves Kanon.

What she cannot say is:

> I want to continue singing after graduation.

That contrast is revealing.

Keke is emotionally unashamed when affection points outward. She is much more guarded when desire would require asking something for herself.

She then says that spending time with Kanon in Shanghai makes her very happy. The voice is warm and personal. The conversation could have remained a celebration of their relationship.

Instead, Keke turns toward obligation.

---

# VI. Keke’s theory of repayment

Keke explains that her parents kindly allowed her to go to Japan.

At first they worried, but they recognized her effort and permitted her to do what she loved for three years.

She concludes:

> 「大学に行けば、お父さんもお母さんも安心する」
> “If I attend university, Father and Mother will be reassured.”

> 「両親への恩返しにもなる」
> “It will also repay my parents.”

Therefore she intends to attend university in Beijing.

This is not a false concern. Keke genuinely loves her parents and understands that international schooling, uncertainty, and artistic aspiration create real family anxiety.

The distortion lies in how she defines repayment.

Her reasoning is:

> My parents gave me freedom for three years.
> Freedom created a debt.
> Repayment means returning to the path that appears safest to them.
> Continuing the dream would ask for too much.

Keke treats trust as a finite allowance rather than a relationship that can adapt to the person she became while using it.

---

# VII. 「スクールアイドルと出会っていなければ」

Keke attempts to make the university path sound natural:

> 「スクールアイドルと出会っていなければ、もともと進んでいた道ですよ」
> “If I had never encountered school idols, it is the path I originally would have taken.”

The sentence is logically true and existentially misleading.

She invokes a hypothetical Keke who never met school idols as though that untransformed person still has greater authority over her future than the actual Keke who:

-   moved to Japan;

-   met Kanon;

-   founded Liella!;

-   competed in Love Live;

-   mentored juniors;

-   sang before enormous audiences;

-   and now stands at the center of a school-idol community.


Keke is trying to restore an earlier timeline after the discovery that made restoration impossible.

The counterfactual becomes a tool of self-denial:

> This was always the responsible path.
> School idols were merely a temporary detour.

But the entire series has shown that the detour formed her adult self.

---

# VIII. 「可可の青春はここまで」

Keke says:

> 「可可の青春はここまで」
> “Keke’s youth ends here.”

And:

> 「今年が最後デスから」
> “Because this is my final year.”

The word **青春 / youth** is doing a great deal of defensive work.

By calling school idols “youth,” Keke can treat them as:

-   beautiful;

-   meaningful;

-   emotionally real;

-   but naturally temporary.


The category lets her preserve the past without permitting it to make claims upon the future.

This is more sophisticated than simply saying the dream failed. Keke is not denying that school idols mattered. She is placing them inside a culturally acceptable sealed period:

> school life was the time for dreams;
> adulthood is the time for responsibility.

That is precisely the boundary Kanon later refuses.

---

# IX. Keke’s center becomes a farewell performance

Keke says she will show her family the most radiant version of herself.

On the surface, this aligns perfectly with MoeMoe’s plan: the parents will see Keke shine and understand why she should continue.

But Keke means something different:

> Let them see that their gift produced something beautiful.
> Let me finish beautifully.
> Let the final image be worthy of the freedom they gave me.

Keke wants to turn the stage into a thank-you rather than a request.

That is emotionally generous but personally evasive. She would rather present her parents with a completed, polished memory than ask them to accompany her into uncertainty.

---

# X. Wien sees through the farewell

Before the performance, Wien tells Keke:

> 「もっと笑って」
> “Smile more.”

> 「笑顔、硬すぎるわよ」
> “Your smile is much too stiff.”

Keke claims she is merely concentrating.

Wien then asks directly:

> 「北京の大学、行くの？」
> “Are you going to a university in Beijing?”

There is something especially appropriate about Wien being the one who confronts this decision before Kanon does.

Wien knows what it means for:

-   family expectation;

-   musical vocation;

-   education;

-   and personal legitimacy


to become entangled.

But she responds very differently from her Season 2 self.

She says:

> 「私は見たいわ」
> “I want to see it.”

> 「可可がステージに立ち続ける姿を」
> “I want to see Keke continue standing onstage.”

This is an extraordinary line for Wien.

The girl who once dismissed the Love Live audience as worthless now speaks as a prospective audience member.

She does not say:

-   Keke has sufficient technical ability;

-   continuing would improve the competitive level;

-   abandoning song would waste talent;

-   Keke should prove something.


She simply says that she wants to keep watching Keke perform.

That is relational artistic desire in its clearest form:

> another person’s continued performance has become something Wien personally values.

Kanon’s philosophy of song is no longer merely something Wien understands intellectually. It has begun altering what she wants from other performers.

---

# XI. Wien’s voice in this episode: resistance as ritual, concern as clarity

Wien begins the episode with:

> 「Liella!は敵よ」

She also complains when the three-person group attends Liella!’s event:

> 「まったく、なんで私がここに？」

Tomari asks whether she wants to return.

Wien answers that leaving would make the trip to Shanghai meaningless. She has even bought a light stick the previous day.

Tomari catches the contradiction:

> 「『Liella!は敵』では？」
> “‘Liella! is the enemy,’ wasn’t it?”

Wien does not resolve the contradiction verbally because it has already been resolved behaviorally.

This is a mature version of her tsun pattern:

> declare the boundary;
> cross it voluntarily;
> produce a practical excuse;
> refuse to name attachment.

But when she speaks to Keke about continuing, the usual insulation largely disappears.

> 「私は見たいわ」

The feminine assertion **〜わ** remains characteristic, but the content is plain and vulnerable. She states her own desire without pretending it is objective judgment.

That is a notable advance from the 「ふん」-mediated emotional repairs seen in Season 3, Episode 2. Here she does not need a huff to hide that she has been affected. She tells Keke directly that her future performance matters.

---

# XII. Kanon learns the truth

Kanon is called aside and told that Keke has decided to attend university rather than pursue singing.

Her immediate question is:

> 「せっかく、好きなことと出会えたのに、どうして……」
> “After finally finding something she loves, why…?”

The line carries genuine incomprehension because Kanon now understands love of song as something that can change form but should not be artificially terminated.

This is the matured version of the girl from Season 1 who thought a failed audition meant singing was over.

Kanon has learned:

-   one route can close;

-   another can appear;

-   the underlying love remains authoritative.


She cannot accept Keke’s attempt to turn the underlying love into a completed school memory.

---

# XIII. The confrontation: Keke expects Kanon to understand self-denial

Kanon asks:

> 「進路、本当にいいの？」
> “Are you truly all right with this future?”

Keke insists that they have already discussed it and that the decision is final.

Kanon says:

> 「可可ちゃん、自分にうそついてるよ」
> “Keke-chan, you’re lying to yourself.”

Keke responds:

> 「うるさいデス」
> “Be quiet.”

The delivery is sharply different from her comic or excited 「デス」. The ending strikes hard, and her voice loses its usual melodic buoyancy.

Then:

> 「かのんなら分かってくれると思ってたのに」
> “I thought you, of all people, would understand.”

This line exposes why Kanon’s resistance hurts.

Keke believes she and Kanon share the experience of loving something while accepting limitation. She may also remember Kanon’s initial refusal of Vienna—her choice to remain with Liella! rather than pursue the elite opportunity.

Keke expected Kanon to validate the beautiful self-sacrifice:

> Sometimes love must become gratitude and end.

But Kanon has changed. She has decided to study singing in Vienna after graduation. She is no longer confusing loyalty to the present group with refusal to develop beyond it.

Kanon can now recognize in Keke the self-denial she had nearly enacted herself.

---

# XIV. The return of Keke’s foundational words

Kanon asks:

> 「好きなことを頑張ることに、おしまいなんてあるの？」
> “Can working hard at something you love really have an ending?”

The line is a deliberate return to Season 1, Episode 1.

Kanon had said:

> singing was over;
> she lacked talent;
> she would stop.

Keke answered in shock:

> 「おしまいなんてあるんデスカ」
> “Can something like that really have an ending?”

That question reopened Kanon’s future.

Now Kanon says:

> 「可可ちゃんが私に教えてくれた、私の宝物にしている言葉」
> “Those are the words Keke-chan taught me—the words I treasure.”

This may be the single most important sentence in the episode.

Keke’s voice has become part of Kanon’s inner life.

The earlier episodes have repeatedly shown this process:

-   Keke hears Kanon’s voice and names it wonderful.

-   Kanon begins singing again.

-   Kanon internalizes Keke’s refusal of finality.

-   Kanon uses that philosophy to recruit and guide others.

-   Keke loses access to her own philosophy.

-   Kanon returns it.


The words no longer belong only to their original speaker.

They have become relational inheritance.

---

# XV. 「可可ちゃんが自分にうそをつく姿なんて、私見たくない」

Kanon says:

> 「可可ちゃんが自分にうそをつく姿なんて、私見たくない」
> “I don’t want to see Keke-chan lying to herself.”

This parallels Wien’s earlier:

> 「私は見たいわ。可可がステージに立ち続ける姿を」

The two girls articulate positive and negative versions of the same desire.

Wien:

> I want to continue seeing your truthful stage self.

Kanon:

> I do not want to watch you erase that self.

Their convergence is important. Kanon and Wien differ profoundly in personality and artistic philosophy, but Keke’s situation reveals that both have learned to treat another person’s performance as something worthy of protection.

---

# XVI. Kanon announces Vienna

Kanon tells Keke:

> 「私は卒業したら、ウィーンに歌を勉強しに行く」
> “After graduation, I’m going to Vienna to study singing.”

This statement changes the moral force of the confrontation.

Kanon is no longer telling Keke to pursue risk while she herself chooses the safety of familiar relationships.

She is accepting that:

-   Liella! matters;

-   Yuigaoka restored her voice;

-   school-idol time is precious;

-   and her older global dream still deserves a future.


Then:

> 「だから可可ちゃんも、まっすぐ突き進んでほしいよ」
> “So I want you to keep moving straight forward too.”

The repetition of **突き進む / push forward** also answers MoeMoe’s earlier certainty that Keke will continue on the path of song.

Kanon, MoeMoe, and Wien all see continuity.

Keke alone is trying to narrate an ending.

---

# XVII. Keke’s voice finally breaks

Keke says only:

> 「かのん……」

Then her emotional control collapses.

Kanon observes:

> 「そんな顔じゃ、ステージ立てないよ」
> “You can’t go onstage with a face like that.”

Keke replies:

> 「かのんのせいデス」
> “It’s Kanon’s fault.”

This is not accusation in the ordinary sense. It is an admission that Kanon has made the self-protective story unsustainable.

Keke’s voice here is considerably smaller and less presentational than her usual persona. The 「デス」 survives, but it can no longer restore brightness or comic rhythm. Her characteristic speech marker becomes the fragile remainder of composure.

Kanon apologizes.

Then immediately turns feeling into action.

---

# XVIII. Kanon as producer: create the condition in which Keke can choose honestly

Kanon says:

> 「私、やる」
> “I’ll do it.”

> 「可可ちゃんが、一番祝福される状況を私が作る」
> “I will create the situation in which Keke-chan is blessed and celebrated most fully.”

This is a mature leadership statement.

Kanon cannot:

-   decide Keke’s future;

-   command her parents;

-   erase financial or educational uncertainty;

-   guarantee a professional singing career.


What she can do is construct an event in which Keke’s continued song becomes publicly visible as something other people desire.

The key word is **祝福 / blessing, celebration**.

Keke currently imagines that continuing would burden or disappoint her parents.

Kanon wants to surround the choice with audible evidence that Keke’s song is not selfish isolation. It is already part of other people’s happiness.

This is performance used not as coercion but as **social recognition**.

---

# XIX. Wien and Tomari join without needing to endorse Liella!

Kanon recruits Wien and Tomari:

> 「やろう」
> “Let’s do it.”

> 「私たちに今できること」
> “What we can do right now.”

Wien gives the familiar resistance:

> 「面倒はごめんよ」
> “I don’t want trouble.”

But she participates.

Tomari participates too.

This matters for both.

## Wien

She is no longer merely crossing the rival boundary privately. She uses TomaKanoTe’s public identity to support a Liella! member.

Rivalry has become compatible with care.

## Tomari

She has spent the season asking whether school idols possess value beyond measurable return.

Here she contributes to an act whose benefit cannot be quantified:

-   give Keke courage;

-   give her parents an experience;

-   create communal affirmation;

-   make an honest choice psychologically possible.


Tomari’s behavior is already moving beyond her stated theory of utility.

---

# XX. TomaKanoTe borrows the stage

The trio introduces itself:

> 「日本からやって来ました、トマカノーテです」
> “We are TomaKanoTe, visiting from Japan.”

They tell the audience that time remains before Liella!’s performance and ask for one favor:

> call Keke’s name loudly;
> Shanghai is her home;
> summon her to the stage with their voices.

This is an extraordinary reversal of ordinary idol-stage hierarchy.

The performers are not asking:

> Look at us.
> Approve us.
> Remember our names.

They use their visibility to redirect attention toward someone else.

Kanon’s public-address voice is warm, clear, and inclusive. She frames the audience not as passive spectators but as co-authors of the moment.

Keke will not merely walk onto the stage.

Her hometown will **call her into it**.

That public summons answers her private belief that continuing to sing would be an imposition.

The audience’s response says:

> Your presence is wanted.

---

# XXI. Wien’s invitation to the audience

After the call, Wien says:

> 「私たちのステージも、今度見てよね」
> “Come see our stage next time too.”

This is another major change in Wien’s audience relationship.

In Season 2 she treated an audience that judged her unfavorably as worthless.

In Season 3, Episode 2 she learned to address even a small audience sincerely after reputational damage had made the formal goal impossible.

Here she asks an audience to return.

She now imagines spectatorship as an ongoing relationship:

> we helped create this moment for Keke;
> perhaps you will later come hear us too.

It is promotional, but not merely transactional. Wien is learning to want reciprocal continuity with listeners.

---

# XXII. Chisato’s one-night proposal

Backstage, Chisato proposes:

> 「今日だけ特別、かのんちゃんと一緒にライブしない？」
> “Just for today, as something special, why don’t we perform together with Kanon-chan?”

The others initially object:

-   the groups are rivals;

-   they have not prepared emotionally;

-   the structure has been separate.


Chisato answers:

> they are in Shanghai now;
> this is Keke’s homecoming;
> the highest moment should belong to all of Yuigaoka’s school idols.

Her leadership function is fully visible.

Kanon creates the public summons.

Chisato converts that emotional opening into a viable ensemble structure.

This division of labor is characteristic:

-   Kanon identifies and articulates meaning.

-   Chisato makes the group capable of embodying it.


The eleven-person stage does not permanently erase the two-club distinction. Chisato explicitly calls it a special one-day arrangement.

That is important. Relation does not require premature institutional merger.

They can remain rivals and still create one shared work when the moment asks for it.

---

# XXIII. 「結ヶ丘のスクールアイドル全員で」

The invitation culminates in:

> 「最高の瞬間を」
> “The greatest moment…”

> 「結ヶ丘のスクールアイドル全員で」
> “…with every school idol from Yuigaoka.”

This is the episode’s ensemble thesis.

The girls are not yet formally one group.

But they share:

-   a school;

-   a history;

-   Kanon’s recovered voice;

-   Keke’s founding enthusiasm;

-   and responsibility for what school idolhood at Yuigaoka will become.


The performance therefore anticipates Liella!’s eventual reunion without pretending the intervening separation was meaningless.

The split created:

-   Wien’s recovery of joy;

-   Tomari’s practical investigation;

-   Kanon’s development as a cross-boundary mentor;

-   and a rival structure capable of helping Liella! from outside it.


The reunion is powerful because difference has produced something before being recombined.

---

# XXIV. “絶対的LOVER” as Keke’s answer

The insert song’s lyrical language is perfectly suited to Keke.

Its core vocabulary is not:

-   proof;

-   duty;

-   repayment;

-   educational security;

-   endings.


It is:

-   love;

-   gratitude;

-   response;

-   presence;

-   enjoyment;

-   continuation.


The opening declares:

> 「大好きさ、ねぇ大好きって」
> “I love you—hey, I want to say that I love you.”

> 「伝えたいんだ」
> “I want to tell you.”

> 「最大級にアイシテル！」
> “I love you to the absolute maximum!”

This is Keke’s ordinary emotional mode expanded into group song. She has always been the member least ashamed to say that she loves:

-   school idols;

-   Kanon’s voice;

-   Liella!;

-   the people beside her.


The song argues that this directness is not childish excess. It is artistic power.

---

# XXV. Gratitude becomes exchange, not repayment

The lyrics say:

> 「笑顔見えた！ いつもありがと！」
> “I saw your smile! Thank you, always!”

Keke’s pre-performance model of gratitude was:

> My parents gave me three years.
> I should repay them by abandoning uncertainty.

The song offers another model:

> Gratitude is not the termination of relationship.
> It is an ongoing exchange of smiles, voices, and presence.

The performers thank the audience.

The audience answers.

The response is itself described as a love letter:

> 「レスポンスはラブレターなんだ」
> “The response is a love letter.”

This is the opposite of debt settlement.

Nobody completes the exchange and walks away free of obligation. Each response creates another reason to continue.

---

# XXVI. Shanghai enters the language of the song

The song incorporates:

> 「來、來、來！」
> “Come, come, come!”

> 「我愛你！」
> “I love you!”

and the call language:

> 「打Call！」

Keke’s Chinese identity is not treated as something she must leave at home in order to perform Japanese school-idol culture.

The stage instead folds languages and performance traditions together.

This is crucial because Keke’s original journey to Japan could be interpreted as movement away from home toward an imported dream.

The Shanghai stage demonstrates that the dream can return home transformed without ceasing to belong there.

Keke is neither:

-   the Chinese girl temporarily playing at Japanese school idols;

-   nor the Japanese school idol who must suppress her home identity.


The song lets both worlds answer one another.

---

# XXVII. 「今はただ今に夢中で」

The lyrics say:

> 「今はただ今に夢中で」
> “Right now, we are simply absorbed in right now.”

This line could sound like confirmation of Keke’s belief that school idols belong only to temporary youth.

But the following lyrical movement changes its meaning:

> 「ステージ、いつまでも煌めきたいの」
> “I want to keep shining onstage forever.”

> 「最高をもっと更新しようよ！」
> “Let’s keep updating our greatest height!”

The song holds present intensity and future continuity together.

The correct answer is not:

> Ignore the future and enjoy school.

Nor:

> Sacrifice the present for responsible adulthood.

It is:

> Be fully present—and let the truth discovered in that presence continue changing the future.

The word **更新 / update** is especially apt. The highest point is not a completed treasure to preserve unchanged. It can be surpassed.

Keke’s Shanghai performance need not be the perfect end.

It can become a new beginning.

---

# XXVIII. Keke as center of eleven

Visually, the performance surrounds Keke with all ten other Yuigaoka school idols.

The stage uses:

-   saturated candy colors;

-   hearts;

-   bubbles;

-   playful typography;

-   camera-aware gestures;

-   call-and-response framing;

-   bright close-ups;

-   repeated movement between Keke and the surrounding ensemble.


The visual world resembles Keke’s personality more than the severe or elegant performance worlds associated with Wien.

It is:

-   maximal;

-   affectionate;

-   socially open;

-   slightly excessive;

-   joyful without embarrassment.


Keke is not center because she is being separated from the others as singular proof of talent.

She is center because the other ten are organizing their energy around the wish that she recognize how widely her original enthusiasm has spread.

This is perhaps the purest possible Liella! center structure:

> the center is the person whose history the group is collectively making visible.

---

# XXIX. Keke’s parents as audience

The episode does not give the parents an explicit spoken verdict.

That evidentiary distinction matters.

What the bundle supports is:

-   they attend;

-   they watch Keke;

-   they see the audience respond;

-   their expressions soften;

-   they witness their daughter at the center of an eleven-person performance;

-   they see that her song has become part of a large community.


The episode strongly implies recognition and emotional blessing.

It does not yet provide a detailed verbal negotiation about:

-   university;

-   professional training;

-   finances;

-   or the exact conditions under which Keke will continue singing.


This is appropriate.

The performance cannot solve every practical question.

It can change the emotional premises under which those questions will be discussed.

---

# XXX. 「可可は、今一番幸せデスよ」

After the performance, Keke begins:

> 「可可は……」

There is a pause.

Then:

> 「可可は、今一番幸せデスよ」
> “Keke is the happiest she has ever been right now.”

The line unites several versions of her voice.

She refers to herself by name, as she often does in her high-energy public speech. But the delivery is softer and more fragile than OniNatsu-like branding or ordinary comic enthusiasm.

The familiar 「デスよ」 remains, but it no longer creates upward momentum. It lands gently, almost as reassurance directed toward:

-   her parents;

-   MoeMoe;

-   Kanon;

-   the group;

-   and perhaps herself.


The sentence’s **今 / now** is important.

It does not guarantee that every future decision is solved.

It states an undeniable present fact:

> the path her parents permitted has made her profoundly happy.

That fact now has to be included in any responsible account of her future.

---

# XXXI. Kanon’s final reply

Kanon says:

> 「上海で歌えてよかったね」
> “I’m glad you were able to sing in Shanghai.”

Keke answers:

> 「はい」
> “Yes.”

Kanon does not say:

-   I saved you.

-   Now you have to become a professional singer.

-   Your parents certainly agree.

-   Everything is settled.


She names the completed experience and leaves Keke ownership of what follows.

This is mature accompaniment.

Kanon creates the conditions for truth to become audible, but she does not claim the authority to write the next chapter for Keke.

---

# XXXII. Cumulative voice profile: Tang Keke

This episode is the most substantial refinement of Keke’s profile since Season 1.

## Public school-idol voice

Keke’s familiar mode remains:

-   bright;

-   quick;

-   high-energy;

-   full of expressive pitch movement;

-   direct in praise and enthusiasm;

-   marked by stylized 「デス」「マス」 endings.


This voice can still animate the entire group.

## Direct-affection voice

Her:

> 「かのんはいつも優しいデスね」
> 「大好きデス」

is simple, warm, and unembarrassed.

Keke is extraordinarily comfortable expressing love for another person compared with most of the cast.

## Filial-responsibility voice

When discussing her parents and university, the brightness narrows.

Her phrases become:

-   longer;

-   more causally organized;

-   less spontaneous;

-   quieter;

-   unusually final.


She sounds as though she has rehearsed the justification until it resembles acceptance.

## Self-ending voice

> 「可可の青春はここまで」
> 「今年が最後デスから」

The lines retain her characteristic speech style, but the vocal energy runs downward rather than outward. The persona no longer produces hope; it stabilizes resignation.

## Defensive anger

> 「うるさいデス」
> 「かのんなら分かってくれると思ってたのに」

The 「デス」 becomes hard and percussive. Her anger is strongest because Kanon’s interpretation is correct.

## Crying voice

Once Kanon returns Keke’s own words, Keke can no longer preserve polished reasoning. Her speech contracts to Kanon’s name and the accusation:

> 「かのんのせいデス」

This is one of her most childlike and relationally dependent moments.

## Performance voice

“絶対的LOVER” restores Keke’s natural outward movement:

-   love is declared;

-   gratitude seeks response;

-   Chinese and Japanese coexist;

-   the audience becomes a partner;

-   the present opens toward future continuation.


## Final voice

> 「可可は、今一番幸せデスよ」

The line integrates daughter, idol, foreign student, founder, and center.

## Updated core thesis

> **Keke’s vocal gift is the fearless declaration of love, but she struggles to apply that fearlessness to her own future. Her arc here is learning that gratitude toward her family does not require silencing the person their trust allowed her to become.**

---

# XXXIII. Cumulative voice profile: Shibuya Kanon

## Listener voice

Kanon initially lets Keke explain her entire reasoning. Her responses are brief and gentle rather than immediately corrective.

She needs to understand whether Keke is genuinely choosing university or performing acceptance.

## Moral-recognition voice

Once she understands, Kanon becomes unusually direct:

> 「自分にうそついてるよ」

The delivery is firm because she is no longer willing to confuse kindness with validation of self-erasure.

## Inherited-voice function

Kanon speaks Keke’s old words back to her.

This is perhaps the clearest demonstration that Kanon’s mature voice is collectively authored. She does not simply generate wisdom independently. She preserves what others gave her and returns it when needed.

## Self-authored future voice

> 「私は卒業したら、ウィーンに歌を勉強しに行く」

The statement is clean and undecorated. Kanon now speaks her own long-term artistic future without apology.

## Producer voice

> 「可可ちゃんが、一番祝福される状況を私が作る」

Kanon does not only comfort. She designs:

-   a stage intervention;

-   an audience ritual;

-   a temporary cross-group ensemble;

-   and the public conditions in which Keke can be seen truthfully.


## Public-address voice

Her TomaKanoTe introduction treats the Shanghai audience as collaborators. She asks them to summon Keke rather than merely watch her.

## Updated core thesis

> **Kanon’s mature leadership consists of preserving other people’s truest words, recognizing when they have lost access to them, and building a situation in which they can hear those words again through experience.**

---

# XXXIV. Cumulative voice profile: Wien Margarete

## Rival-boundary voice

> 「Liella!は敵よ」

Wien still uses short categorical assertions to preserve separation.

## Tsun rationalization

She buys a light stick and attends Liella!’s event, then claims returning would make the Shanghai trip meaningless.

Her actions have moved beyond the worldview her language continues to defend.

## Smile-coaching voice

Her criticism of Keke’s stiff smile is practical and perceptive. Wien notices the mismatch between a performance face and the emotion underneath it.

## Direct-desire voice

> 「私は見たいわ。可可がステージに立ち続ける姿を」

This is one of Wien’s least defended relational statements.

She no longer needs to translate care into artistic hierarchy.

## Cooperative public voice

She joins TomaKanoTe’s support intervention and invites the audience to see their future stage as well.

She is beginning to understand artistic community as ongoing exchange rather than a tribunal that declares rank.

## Updated core thesis

> **Wien’s growth is becoming audible whenever she replaces judgment with a first-person desire: not “Keke ought to continue because she is talented,” but “I want to keep seeing her.”**

---

# XXXV. Cumulative voice profile: Onitsuka Tomari

## Continuing audit voice

Tomari says:

> 「姉者の発言が本気かどうか、それをこの目で確認したいだけです」

She still frames her participation as evidence gathering.

## Observational restraint

She remains less emotionally volatile than the others, even in unfamiliar Shanghai surroundings. Her sparse diet, composure, and practical comments extend her controlled self-management into the body.

## Exposure of Wien’s contradiction

Her:

> 「『Liella!は敵』では？」

is delivered as factual inconsistency detection rather than teasing exuberance.

## Action beyond theory

Tomari nevertheless joins Kanon’s plan to help Keke.

The action has no obvious financial return or durable credential. It serves another person’s emotional truth.

## Updated core thesis

> **Tomari continues to call herself an evaluator, but her participation is turning evaluation into responsibility. She increasingly acts to protect the human value she has not yet fully admitted exists.**

---

# XXXVI. Cumulative voice profile: Arashi Chisato

Chisato’s most important action is the invitation that creates the eleven-person stage.

She does not dissolve the separate groups permanently. She identifies a moment in which keeping the boundary intact would diminish the meaning available to everyone.

Her leadership remains situational and relational:

> rules exist;
> rivalries matter;
> preparation matters;
> but a structure must sometimes bend to serve the people for whom it exists.

Her voice makes the proposal sound simple, even though it reorganizes the episode’s entire performance plan.

## Updated core thesis

> **Chisato is becoming the ensemble architect who knows when difference should be preserved and when the moment requires everyone to move as one body.**

---

# XXXVII. Smaller ensemble refinements

## Mei

Mei openly wants Shanghai audiences to recognize Keke’s appeal. Her rough voice now carries senior admiration and protective pride rather than hidden fandom.

## Shiki

Her immediate **“Me too”** compresses full agreement into the smallest possible verbal unit. Her established speech identity remains perfectly consistent even in an emotionally significant center decision.

## Kinako

Kinako treats Keke’s homecoming with uncomplicated excitement. She helps make the Shanghai stage feel like celebration rather than tribunal.

## Natsumi

Her:

> 「ついに私たちも世界進出ですの」

shows how readily she translates the Shanghai event into expanded reach. Her creator persona now serves Liella!’s collective excitement rather than extracting value from it.

## Sumire

Sumire supports Keke’s center and recognizes that Keke’s family will be pleased. Her entertainment-world instincts align with the obvious dramaturgy: the hometown founder should stand where the audience can see her most clearly.

## Ren

Ren helps formalize the center decision and the group’s total commitment to the festival. Her institutional voice continues functioning as confirmation and orderly coordination.

---

# XXXVIII. Formal structure: from separation to public convergence

The episode begins with separated systems:

-   Liella!;

-   TomaKanoTe;

-   Keke’s Japanese life;

-   Keke’s Chinese family;

-   youth;

-   adulthood;

-   art;

-   responsibility.


Its visual and narrative movement gradually eliminates absolute separation without erasing difference.

First, the groups occupy Shanghai separately.

Then Kanon and Keke speak privately.

Wien crosses from rival spectator to personal advocate.

Kanon and Keke’s argument makes Keke’s private conflict visible.

TomaKanoTe uses its stage for Liella!.

Liella! invites TomaKanoTe into its stage.

The parents enter the audience.

The audience calls Keke.

Chinese enters the Japanese-language song.

All eleven girls perform together.

The episode does not resolve identity by choosing one side.

It constructs a stage large enough to hold the sides together.

---

# XXXIX. Performance as family communication

The parents do not receive a conventional persuasive presentation.

Nobody tells them:

-   projected career earnings;

-   educational alternatives;

-   training options;

-   industry plans.


Instead, they witness:

-   Keke chosen as center;

-   ten other girls organizing themselves around her;

-   a hometown crowd answering her voice;

-   the daughter they supported radiating joy;

-   and a performance whose lyrics explicitly connect gratitude, love, response, and future continuation.


That is not sufficient for every practical decision.

But it communicates something practical language had hidden:

> Singing is no longer one activity Keke enjoys.
> It is the social world in which she has become most fully alive.

---

# Final synthesis

Season 3, Episode 6 is not simply about persuading Keke’s parents to let her continue singing.

Its deeper conflict is inside Keke.

She believes adulthood should prove that she appreciated her parents’ generosity by placing the dream safely in the past.

Kanon tells her that this is not gratitude.

It is self-betrayal dressed as gratitude.

The episode’s decisive move is to make Keke hear the long afterlife of her own original faith.

In Season 1, Keke asked Kanon:

> Can something you love really have an ending?

Kanon preserved that question.

It became part of how she understood:

-   singing;

-   failure;

-   Mei and Shiki;

-   Natsumi;

-   Wien;

-   her own future in Vienna.


When Keke forgets, Kanon says:

> Those words are my treasure.

That is what Liella! has become at its best.

Not a group in which one inspirational leader gives everyone else meaning.

A group in which words circulate, are preserved, mature inside other people, and return when the original speaker needs them.

Wien says she wants to keep seeing Keke onstage.

Kanon says she refuses to watch Keke lie to herself.

MoeMoe brings the family to see.

Chisato brings all eleven performers together.

The audience calls Keke’s name.

Keke finally says:

> 「可可は、今一番幸せデスよ」

The episode does not yet reduce the future to an easy answer.

It establishes the truth that any future answer must respect:

> **The dream is no longer a temporary exception to Keke’s responsible life.
> It is one of the things that made the life recognizably hers.**

---

# *Love Live! Superstar!!* — Season 3, Episode 12

## Final audio-integrated analysis and completed running voice profiles

I reviewed the complete Season 3, Episode 12 analysis bundle: approximately 23:42 of stereo audio, 393 Japanese subtitle cues, 743 retained episode frames, and 38 contact sheets.

The broader series analysis described *Superstar!!* as a story about **beginnings learning how to become inheritances**. Love Live Superstar Understandi… This finale realizes that thesis with unusual precision:

> **Liella! succeeds not merely when Kanon recovers her voice, nor when the group wins Love Live, but when the founding members can leave without the voice, group, school, or dream disappearing with them.**

The episode is about graduation, but it repeatedly rejects the idea that graduation is equivalent to erasure.

The founders leave the school.
Their daily proximity ends.
The rooftop changes owners.
The group’s configuration changes.
Kanon goes to Vienna.
Keke returns to Shanghai.
Ren, Chisato, and Sumire move into their own futures.

Yet their voices remain in:

-   the younger members’ speech;

-   the principles they repeat;

-   the school’s institutional language;

-   the memories attached to physical places;

-   the new Liella!’s distribution of roles;

-   and a song that begins with five voices and expands into eleven.


The most accurate formulation of the finale is:

> **The original Liella! does not survive by remaining unchanged.
> It survives by becoming audible in people who are no longer the original Liella!.**

---

# I. Margarete’s 「ふん」 audit

I did **not** find a confident new Margarete 「ふん」 in this episode.

Two moments could easily be misidentified:

-   At approximately **03:37**, the clearly subtitled

    > 「フン！ 私についてこられるかしら？」
    > is **Sumire**, speaking to Keke.

-   At approximately **11:26**, the small final

    > 「ふん！」
    > belongs to **Kinako**, as she declares that she will ask the seniors to repeat a year.


Margarete does use closely related defensive vocal behavior:

> 「泣いてない！」
> “I’m not crying!”

and later:

> 「言われなくたって」
> “You don’t have to tell me.”

Both serve the same broad function as her recurring huffs—concealing that she has been emotionally affected—but she does not punctuate them with a distinct, confidently attributable 「ふん」.

That absence is itself mildly revealing. In earlier episodes, the huff often repaired a leak in her emotional defenses. Here, her feelings repeatedly escape too completely for that small repair mechanism:

-   she openly reassures Kanon;

-   she cries at graduation;

-   she accepts responsibility for Liella!;

-   and she responds forcefully to the seniors’ farewell principle.


Her defenses remain, but they have become ordinary embarrassment rather than an entire philosophy of superiority.

---

# II. Kanon packing: the protagonist becomes frightened in an ordinary way

The episode begins not with a triumphant winner preparing for international greatness, but with Kanon failing to fit her possessions into a suitcase.

Her speech is full of stretched endings and unsettled fragments:

> 「どうしよう…」
> 「こんなに持っていけないよぉ…」
> 「こんなの入るわけないよ～！」

Her voice rises and elongates around the impossible packing problem. This is a noticeably younger, more domestic Kanon than the measured leader who confronted Wien or guided Natsumi.

The scene deliberately makes her Vienna anxiety mundane:

-   bedding;

-   food;

-   unfamiliar rooms;

-   strangers;

-   what happens if something goes wrong;

-   whether familiar café au lait will be available.


Kanon is not afraid that she lacks the artistic ability to study abroad. That question has largely been resolved.

She is afraid of living somewhere unfamiliar.

This is healthy development. Her fear is no longer:

> My body will betray me, and therefore I am not permitted to sing.

It is:

> I am leaving home, and leaving home is frightening.

The dream is not being invalidated by the fear. The fear is now allowed to accompany the dream.

---

# III. Margarete reassures Kanon using her own migration story

Margarete initially corrects Kanon in her familiar manner:

> 「昨日言ったでしょ？」
> 「そんなに必要ないって」

The delivery is clipped and practical. She has already explained that her sister will arrange Kanon’s room and meals.

But when Kanon says that she will know nobody there, Margarete changes register:

> 「私が日本に来た時も、知り合いなんて一人もいなかったけど？」
> “When I came to Japan, I didn’t know anyone either.”

Then:

> 「それでも、私、今とても楽しいわ」
> 「だから大丈夫よ。かのんなら」
> “Even so, I’m very happy now. So you’ll be all right—because you’re Kanon.”

The correction voice is firmer and more energetic; the reassurance is softer, less percussive, and composed of longer phrases. Margarete stops prescribing and starts testifying.

This is one of the strongest reversals of her entire arc.

Season 2 Margarete understood international movement through:

-   elite institutions;

-   family judgment;

-   examination;

-   proof;

-   and hierarchy.


Season 3 Margarete can now describe migration through:

-   loneliness;

-   new relationships;

-   ordinary happiness;

-   and confidence that another person can become connected too.


She has become capable of saying:

> I arrived alone, and relation made the unfamiliar place livable.

That is Kanon’s artistic philosophy translated into Margarete’s biography.

## Margarete and Aria as Kanon’s temporary family structure

Aria agrees immediately:

> 「うん！」

Kanon then embraces both of them:

> 「マルガレーテちゃん…ありあ…だ～い好き！」

Margarete is no longer merely the rival who will inherit Liella!. She occupies Kanon’s home, helps at the café, advises her on Vienna, and gets pulled into a family hug beside Aria.

The episode begins by showing that Kanon’s departure is possible partly because Margarete has crossed from rival to domestic continuity.

Margarete will not accompany Kanon to Vienna as originally expected. Instead, she helps make leaving emotionally possible—and remains behind to protect what Kanon leaves.

---

# IV. Kanon’s mother and Aria: home does not romanticize departure

Kanon’s mother responds to her anxiety with dry practicality:

> 「向こうにもあるでしょ？」
> 「なかったら自分で作ってよね」

She does not treat Kanon’s departure as tragic destiny. She assumes Kanon will learn to solve ordinary problems.

Later she says:

> 「ひとりになるのはしょうがないわね」

And, when Kanon wishes Margarete could divide herself into two people:

> 「何ばかなこと言ってるの」

This familial bluntness is important. Liella! members often treat Kanon as:

-   founder;

-   emotional interpreter;

-   great singer;

-   center;

-   or future international artist.


At home, she remains a daughter saying foolish things because she is nervous.

Aria similarly tells her:

> 「じゃあ、お姉ちゃんも前向いていかなきゃだね」

Aria’s speech has none of the ceremonial weight with which Liella! discusses Kanon’s future. She offers a sister’s simple correction:

> The new six are moving forward. You have to do the same.

Home keeps Kanon human at the exact moment the narrative could have turned her into a completed heroic symbol.

---

# V. Keke and Sumire: school-idol love becomes an adult vocation

Kanon calls Keke for advice and discovers her at the same agency as Sumire.

Keke initially attempts an extremely poor deception:

> 「き…奇遇ですね～！」

Her pitch rises and the phrase is stretched with obvious nervousness. The woman capable of declaring enormous dreams before crowds becomes comically transparent when caught hiding something from Kanon.

She then explains her future with increasing steadiness:

> 「『ラブライブ！』は高校生の間だけ輝ける世界」
> “Love Live is a world in which one can shine only during high school.”

But:

> 「この先も歌い続けたい」
> 「ステージに立ちたい」
> “I want to keep singing. I want to keep standing onstage.”

Therefore she has joined an entertainment agency.

She will return to Shanghai, attend university, and work toward returning to singing and dancing onstage. The plan is neither the complete abandonment of her family’s educational expectations nor an impulsive refusal of adulthood.

It is a transnational compromise authored by the mature Keke:

-   education in Shanghai;

-   professional affiliation;

-   continued preparation;

-   and eventual return to the stage.


Season 3, Episode 6 prevented her from sealing school idols inside the category of “youth.” Episode 12 shows her constructing an adult life in which that love persists.

## Keke’s vocal integration

Keke’s characteristic brightness and 「デス」 remain, but they now carry a more concrete future.

Earlier Keke’s exuberance often announced faith:

> School idols are wonderful.
> Kanon’s voice must reach people.
> Our dream cannot end.

Here the same voice presents:

-   university;

-   agency work;

-   practice;

-   auditions;

-   photography schedules;

-   and long-term professional preparation.


Faith has acquired administration.

That becomes comically obvious when she produces Sumire’s packed schedule:

> 「これからの練習メニューデス！」
> 「更に！ これがオーディションの予定」
> 「更に、撮影の予定デス」

Her speech accelerates and regains its missionary rhythm, except the object is now Sumire’s career management.

Keke has transformed from school-idol recruiter into aspiring producer-manager without losing the emotional excess that made her Keke.

---

# VI. Sumire’s 「フン！」: pride as affectionate professional rivalry

Sumire says:

> 「何か負けた気分…！」

Keke answers that they now stand on the same starting line and that she is happy they can work hard together again.

Sumire responds:

> 「フン！ 私についてこられるかしら？」

This huff belongs to Sumire, and its function resembles one of Margarete’s friendlier variants:

> I am pleased by this development.
> I refuse to state that directly.
> Let me convert pleasure into a challenge.

The delivery is theatrical rather than genuinely contemptuous. Keke immediately laughs.

Their relationship has matured to the point that abrasive performance is a stable language of affection. Sumire no longer fears that Keke’s criticism means she is unworthy of center, and Keke no longer fears that Sumire’s pride means she lacks commitment.

They can carry their Season 1 rhythm into professional adulthood.

## The deeper significance

Keke and Sumire once represented competing relationships to idolhood:

-   Keke: faith, devotion, and dream;

-   Sumire: industry, visibility, and professional ambition.


Their shared agency future indicates that those philosophies have become complementary.

Keke supplies relentless belief and planning.

Sumire supplies performance experience, worldly instinct, and the hunger to be chosen.

The pair that spent the first season fighting over whether idolhood was sacred dream or show business will pursue adult entertainment work together.

---

# VII. Kanon refuses to look in on the new six

Aria suggests that Kanon visit the school and watch Margarete practice with the new Liella!.

Kanon says:

> 「そういうの、したくない」
> 「これからは、６人でスタートするんだから」

The delivery is quiet and firm. There is no dramatic renunciation.

Kanon understands that her presence would change the meaning of the rehearsal. The six would become:

> the remaining members being observed by the founder.

Her absence gives them ownership.

This is one of the clearest signs that Kanon has learned to distinguish support from surveillance. She remains interested in them and wants to cheer them on, but she will not make their beginning answer continually to her gaze.

The act is emotionally costly. Immediately afterward she admits:

> 「４月から…また、ひとりか…」

The withdrawal is not effortless detachment. Kanon is choosing the successors’ autonomy while feeling the solitude that choice creates.

---

# VIII. Kanon’s private guitar song: solitude transformed into accompaniment

Kanon begins playing guitar alone in her room.

The lyrics include:

> 「ほんのちょっぴり かなしいときなら」
> “When you’re just a little sad…”

> 「せすじのばして こえをとばせば」
> “Straighten your back and send your voice outward…”

> 「いつでもそばで ひかりをくれた。うた」
> “Song was always beside me and gave me light.”

> 「てをつなごう」
> “Let’s hold hands.”

This is one of the finale’s most important uses of voice.

The arrangement is spare: guitar, Kanon, and the domestic room. Her singing is not functioning as:

-   tournament proof;

-   audition;

-   center performance;

-   institutional evaluation;

-   or formal gift to an audience.


It is how she processes being alone.

That brings the series back to the earliest Kanon, who could sing privately but lost her voice when judged. Yet the meaning of private song has changed.

Early private singing represented a split:

> the true singer exists only when nobody is looking.

This private singing represents integration:

> the public singer, leader, friend, graduate, and frightened daughter can still sing when nobody is looking.

She no longer needs an audience to validate the voice.

She also no longer experiences solitude as proof of abandonment. The song itself contains everyone who taught her what voice could do.

Her private voice is now relational even when physically alone.

---

# IX. The new six: Liella! already has a functioning successor culture

The new Liella! practice scene distributes the future with remarkable efficiency.

## Wien and Tomari: two forms of severity

Tomari says calmly:

> 「私とマルガレーテでメニューを作ったのですが…」

Wien follows:

> 「今までがたるんでいたのよ」
> 「来年はこの６人で優勝しなきゃいけないんだから」

Tomari’s authority is procedural. She reports that they constructed a program and is genuinely puzzled that it seems unusually hard.

Wien’s authority is evaluative and competitive. She declares the old standard inadequate and invokes the necessity of victory.

Their voices remain distinct:

**Tomari:** measured, neutral, explanatory.
**Wien:** projected, categorical, impatient.

Together they form a powerful training apparatus:

> Tomari can systematize the route.
> Wien ensures the destination remains difficult.

## Kinako and Natsumi: comic bodily protest

Kinako collapses into:

> 「疲れたっす～！」
> 「はひぃ～！」

Natsumi cries:

> 「いきなり練習がハードすぎですの～！」

Their speech markers survive physical exhaustion:

-   Kinako’s 「〜っす」;

-   Natsumi’s melodic 「ですの」.


The contrast with Tomari and Wien makes the six sound like an actual group rather than an abstract succession chart. The future Liella! will remain full of incompatible bodily and vocal rhythms.

## Mei: the new club president

Mei declares:

> 「なめんな！ 私はまだまだやれるぞ！」

Shiki immediately asks:

> 「どうする？ 部長」

Mei’s response:

> 「…あ。部長…誰が？」
> 「はい～⁉」

Her voice moves from rough confidence into high, flustered disbelief. As in her original recruitment episode, the rough register proves less stable when she is directly entrusted with something she wants.

Everyone has already chosen her.

Mei protests until she makes one condition:

> 「お前が副部長になるなら、私が部長、引き受けてやる！」

This is a mature version of her earlier confession:

> 「四季が近くにいてくれたら、頑張れそうな気がするんだ」

Mei does not have to disguise Shiki as the reason she cannot pursue a role. She openly makes Shiki’s partnership the condition under which she can accept it.

Connection no longer obstructs agency. It supports it.

## Shiki: rare warmth in minimal language

Shiki answers:

> 「部長の頼みなら…喜んで」

The pause before 「喜んで」 allows a little more feeling through than her usual compressed statements.

She remains characteristically restrained, but the content is openly affectionate:

> If Mei asks, she will gladly stand beside her.

The science-room dyad has become an institutional leadership pair.

## Natsumi: successor producer

Natsumi immediately declares:

> 「プロデュースは夏美にお任せですの～！」

She has found the place where OniNatsu’s media instincts belong.

She will not merely film the next Liella! from outside. She will help shape how it reaches the world from within.

---

# X. The leadership succession is now complete

The six-person Liella! has a surprisingly coherent division of labor:

No one simply becomes “the new Kanon.”

That is one of the finale’s wisest choices.

Liella! survives Kanon not by replacing her with a single equivalent center, but by distributing the functions the founding generation had concentrated around several members.

This is what institutional maturity looks like:

> leadership becomes plural.

---

# XI. The original five reunite because they hear Kanon’s condition

Keke asks Sumire, Ren, and Chisato to help cheer Kanon up.

Chisato enters with a playful slow approach and sudden:

> 「そぉ～…わぁっ！」

Sumire assesses Kanon bluntly:

> 「案外普通ね」

Ren explains:

> 「声を聞いたら、何だって伝わります」
> 「３年間、一緒に過ごしてきたのですから」

This is almost a direct statement of the method behind the audio-integrated analysis.

Ren does not say they understood Kanon from the content of her words. Keke heard her voice on the telephone.

Voice carries:

-   concealed fear;

-   hesitation;

-   forced brightness;

-   loneliness;

-   and what a speaker is trying not to request.


After three years, the original five possess a shared acoustic intimacy. They know one another’s ordinary baselines well enough to hear deviation.

That is what makes a relationship audible.

---

# XII. The rooftop: memory without reenactment

The five return to the rooftop and remember:

-   how bad they initially were;

-   Keke’s lack of stamina;

-   how much they practiced;

-   how frightened they were;

-   and how often they said 「きっと大丈夫」 without evidence.


They recognize something important about that phrase.

It had no factual guarantee behind it.

Yet it gave them:

> 「不思議な力」

Sumire’s practical skepticism and Keke’s belief no longer conflict here. They understand that encouragement need not be a prediction to be effective.

> “It will be all right” was not reliable information.
> It was a relational act that made continued effort possible.

They then describe the path honestly:

> 「迷ったり、考えたり、立ち止まったり…」
> 「嫌なことがあったり、逃げ出したくなったり…」

Their success is not narrated as evidence that every earlier choice was secretly correct.

It is evidence that mistakes, detours, and fear can become part of a path without disqualifying it.

## Kanon refuses one final rehearsal

Someone suggests practicing a little.

Kanon answers:

> 「ううん。ここはもう、新しい『Liella!』のための場所」

This is perhaps the episode’s cleanest act of succession.

They are standing on the rooftop where the original group was formed and trained. Nostalgia makes reenactment tempting.

Kanon declines.

The rooftop is no longer theirs simply because their memories are attached to it.

They can carry the memories away. The physical space must remain usable by people whose future is not an imitation of theirs.

> **Inheritance requires the predecessor to stop occupying every symbolic place.**

---

# XIII. The overnight gathering: leave a song, not graffiti

The original five spend the night at school eating Chisato’s takoyaki hotpot.

The scene allows them to sound less like legendary founders and more like longtime friends:

-   blowing on hot food together;

-   praising Chisato;

-   worrying about whether they have permission;

-   teasing Ren about becoming “RenRen director”;

-   and imagining leaving a mark on the blackboard.


Ren explains that she will attend university in preparation for working in Yuigaoka’s administration. Her institutional arc will continue beyond graduation.

The girl who once treated school as the fixed remnant of her mother’s will now wants to participate in its ongoing governance.

## The song begins as an ethical alternative to possession

Sumire proposes writing:

> 「一期生参上！」

Kanon objects that they should not cause trouble.

The founders want to leave something behind, but the episode refuses a territorial mark. Instead, Kanon suggests:

> 「この５人で、ここで曲を作るっていうのはどう？」

A song is the correct trace because it can be:

-   received;

-   reinterpreted;

-   shared;

-   performed by others;

-   and expanded.


Graffiti says:

> We were here, and this surface belongs to our memory.

Song says:

> We were here, and this can become yours too.

Kanon says there are people to whom she wants to show the live.

The final song is thus born not as self-memorialization, but as a gift to successors.

---

# XIV. Graduation morning: six different ways to cry

The younger members’ emotional performance is almost a compressed vocal anthology.

## Kinako: complete emotional transparency

Kinako openly wails:

> 「先輩たち、いなくなっちゃうっす～！」

She proposes asking them to repeat a year.

Her crying is expansive and unregulated. The same sincerity that made her an accessible recruit now makes separation impossible to disguise.

## Natsumi: persona-assisted control

Natsumi says:

> 「今から泣いてどうするんですの～！」

But she is also audibly affected.

The 「ですの」 persona helps her remain functional. As in the opening of the episode, OniNatsu has become a structure for acting through emotion rather than denying its existence.

## Mei: rough grief

Mei says:

> 「分かってたことだろ…！」

When Shiki points out that Mei is crying too, Mei snaps:

> 「うるせ～！」

Her roughness cannot conceal the tears, but concealment is no longer really the purpose. Everyone knows she is crying. The harsh phrase is simply the form grief naturally takes in Mei’s mouth.

## Shiki: dry observational intimacy

> 「メイ、泣きすぎ」

Shiki does not console Mei through elaborate reassurance. She names what is happening and remains beside her.

The line recalls her first photographs of Mei sobbing over school idols, but the context has transformed completely. Mei’s tears are now shared group knowledge rather than an incriminating secret.

## Wien: practical excuse

Asked why she has attended despite first-years being free to skip the ceremony, Wien says:

> 「暇だったしね」

The excuse is transparently inadequate.

It belongs to her familiar grammar:

> meaningful action
> → minimal practical explanation
> → refusal to name attachment.

## Tomari: the “large task”

Tomari says she has one large task to complete.

Only later do we learn that it is asking Kanon for her ribbon.

She converts a deeply personal request into task language so she can continue approaching it without becoming overwhelmed.

---

# XV. Sumire’s graduation styling: the performer refuses understatement

Sumire arrives with an elaborate hairstyle.

Keke observes:

> 「盛り盛りデス…」

Sumire answers:

> 「卒業式だもん。このくらい当然でしょ？」

This is perfectly consistent with her mature identity.

The girl who once feared being reduced to background refuses to leave Yuigaoka looking visually ordinary. Graduation is a stage, and she will honor it through presentation.

The impulse is no longer desperate.

She does not need the hairstyle to prove that she deserved to be seen. She wants the day to look as important as it feels.

---

# XVI. Ren receives Yuigaoka’s first diploma

Ren is called first:

> 「第１期生 音楽科１組 葉月恋」

The director initially reads the formal text:

> 「あなたは、本校が定めた…」

Then changes it:

> 「いや…あなたの母、葉月花が定めた課程を修了したことを証します」

Ren gives a small:

> 「あっ…」

This tiny voice is more revealing than a speech would have been.

Ren’s cultivated register disappears for a moment. She is not:

-   student council president;

-   school representative;

-   formal respondent;

-   or institutional heir.


She is Hana’s daughter.

Her mother’s dream has become concrete enough to award its first diploma to her own child.

The director then says:

> 「おめでとう」

This completes Ren’s foundational conflict.

She once mistook preservation of her mother’s dream for controlling what the school could become.

Now she graduates from a school whose success came from allowing students to create meanings Hana could never have predetermined.

Inheritance has replaced obedience.

---

# XVII. Kinako’s farewell address: the beginner acquires a public voice

Kinako’s ordinary speech is marked by:

> 「〜っす」

The suffix gives her voice humility, regional texture, and beginner warmth.

At the podium, it disappears.

She adopts a formal ceremonial register:

> 「在校生一同、心よりお祝い申し上げます」

The change is not erasure of personality. It shows expanded capacity. Kinako can now speak as the representative of the continuing school.

She recounts:

-   the founders’ first Love Live;

-   their Tokyo second-place finish;

-   their failure to reach nationals;

-   the frustration she witnessed;

-   and the way they overcame it.


Her address does not reduce the seniors to champions. She identifies their formative defeat as the moment through which their character became instructive.

Then:

> 「そんな先輩方の気持ちを背負って、私たちはこれからも頑張ります」

Kinako accepts inheritance without claiming that the successors will replicate the same path.

They will carry the feeling forward.

## Vocal significance

Kinako spent much of Season 2 sounding as though she was apologizing for occupying space. Here she sustains formal public speech while visibly emotional.

The voice does not cease being soft. It becomes institutionally audible.

The girl who made Liella! reachable to new members becomes the person who speaks for those who remain.

---

# XVIII. Ren’s response: formality becomes communal philosophy

Ren’s answer is delivered in the highly structured register that has defined her from the beginning:

> 「わたくしたちは…」

Complete syntax, deliberate pacing, careful formal vocabulary, and a public cadence remain intact.

But the philosophy inside that voice has completely changed.

She recalls that the first students entered:

-   with no upperclassmen;

-   into a slightly lonely school;

-   with anxiety and hope;

-   and with no road before them.


Then:

> 「その道が、常に正しかったとは思いません」
> “I do not believe that path was always correct.”

> 「時に間違い、時に回り道をして」
> “At times we were mistaken; at times we took detours.”

This is a profound statement coming from Ren.

The early Ren sought correctness through inherited authority. The graduating Ren publicly acknowledges that institutional life is made through fallible movement.

She credits the younger students:

> 「後から入ってきた在校生の皆さんに背中を押され」
> “We were pushed forward by the students who came after us.”

The founders did not simply create Yuigaoka for their juniors.

The juniors also completed the founders’ experience of Yuigaoka.

Finally:

> 「ここにいる全員の一歩一歩が、結ヶ丘をつくっていきます」
> “Every step taken by everyone here will create Yuigaoka.”

And:

> 「それが、羽ばたくわたくしたちの力となっていく」
> “That will become the strength of those of us taking flight.”

Ren’s formal voice no longer defines the institution from above.

It recognizes the institution as an accumulation of everyone’s movement.

This completes her transformation from gatekeeper to steward.

---

# XIX. The group photograph: history made ordinary

After the ceremony, the girls gather for a photograph.

Natsumi calls:

> 「いきますの～！」

Then everyone performs the familiar:

> 「ラブライブ！」

The photograph contains:

-   graduates;

-   continuing members;

-   friends;

-   rivals;

-   and the full historical accumulation of Yuigaoka’s school-idol world.


The image is important because the finale does not preserve the founders primarily through a heroic monument.

It preserves them through a photograph full of:

-   imperfect poses;

-   tears;

-   laughter;

-   and crowded bodies.


The legend becomes personal memory.

---

# XX. The cherry-blossom farewell: emotional styles remain distinct

Everyone begins crying.

Wien insists:

> 「泣いてない！」

Mei immediately points out the obvious contradiction.

Wien’s delivery is forceful and elevated, but it contains none of her earlier contempt. She is not rejecting the value of emotion. She is embarrassed that the emotion is publicly legible.

That is an enormous difference.

Season 2 Wien used strength claims to prevent others from treating her as wounded.

Graduating-scene Wien merely refuses to admit that she is crying while standing among other crying friends.

The defense has become socially ordinary.

---

# XXI. Tomari asks Kanon for her ribbon

Tomari approaches:

> 「かのん先輩！」

Then, after a pause:

> 「リボン…リボン下さい！」

The repetition matters. Her carefully managed syntax briefly fails.

She explains:

> 「ずっと憧れてました…」
> 「スクールアイドルを始めた時から…」

This revises how we understand her Season 3 entrance.

Tomari did not investigate school idols only because she feared for Natsumi. She also admired Kanon from the beginning.

She could not initially integrate that admiration with:

-   her distrust of unprofitable dreams;

-   her fear that school idols would hurt Natsumi;

-   and her own self-presentation as a neutral evaluator.


The ribbon request is the first time she asks Kanon for something that has:

-   no measurable utility;

-   no professional return;

-   no procedural necessity;

-   and enormous personal meaning.


It is a symbolic object.

Tomari is learning that value may reside in a relationship precisely where calculation cannot prove it.

## 「アグリーです！」

Kanon gives her the ribbon and asks:

> 「これからもよろしくね。Liella!のこと」

Tomari answers:

> 「アグリーです！」

Her use of English maintains the compact, slightly technical flavor of her established voice. She does not transform into an emotionally expansive speaker merely because the scene is moving.

She accepts the inheritance in her own linguistic form.

That is better characterization than having her suddenly deliver a conventionally sentimental promise.

---

# XXII. Kanon entrusts Liella! to Wien

Kanon says:

> 「マルガレーテちゃんも、よろしくね」

Wien replies:

> 「言われなくたって」

There is no long declaration and no huff.

The line means:

> You do not need to ask.
> I have already accepted this responsibility.
> I refuse to make the acceptance sound ceremonially tender.

This is the final form of Wien’s tsun grammar.

Season 2 Wien stood outside Liella! demanding proof of superiority.

Season 3 Wien stands inside its future treating protection of the group as self-evident.

She no longer needs to say that Liella! is the enemy.

She no longer needs Kanon’s invitation to define her place.

---

# XXIII. Kinako’s final fear: “You will disappear”

Kinako cannot accept the abstraction of graduation.

She says:

> 「行っちゃったら終わりなんすよね…？」
> 「いなくなっちゃうんすよね⁉」

She imagines entering:

-   the clubroom;

-   the classroom;

-   the familiar school spaces;


and not finding the seniors there.

This is more concrete than saying she will miss them. Kinako fears that places will become evidence of absence.

Kanon answers:

> 「大丈夫。いなくならないよ」
> 「私たちは、ずっと一緒」

Then qualifies what “together” means:

> 「顔が見れなくても、声が聞けなくても」
> “Even when we cannot see one another’s faces or hear one another’s voices…”

> 「一緒に過ごした時間は、その思い出は、なくならない」
> “The time we spent together—the memories—will not disappear.”

This is not a denial of physical separation.

Kanon explicitly acknowledges:

-   faces may not be visible;

-   voices may not be audible;

-   ordinary contact will change.


Continuity is not identical to uninterrupted access.

The relationship has become part of the person carrying it.

This is the same psychological development Episode 11 gave Kanon when she learned that “not alone” did not require everyone to stand physically beside her.

She now teaches that principle to Kinako.

---

# XXIV. The seniors’ final principles and the successors’ answers

The farewell becomes a compressed exchange of inherited philosophies.

## Kanon → Kinako

> 「あなたの歌を響かせて」
> “Let your song resound.”

Kinako answers:

> 「全力で響かせるっす！」
> “I’ll make it resound with everything I have!”

This is the central succession.

Kanon does not ask Kinako to sing Kanon’s song.

She says:

> **your song.**

Kinako, once overwhelmed by the mere possibility of becoming an idol, now answers with total commitment.

## Keke → Shiki

> 「自分の大好きを大切にするのデス」
> “Treasure the thing you love.”

The response:

> 「大好きを心の中に！」
> “Keep what you love inside your heart!”

Keke’s school-idol faith passes toward one of the cast’s quietest speakers.

Shiki’s love has always been less loudly expressed than Keke’s:

-   love for Mei;

-   interest in school idols;

-   pleasure in participation;

-   attachment to the group.


The inheritance is not “become as loud as Keke.”

It is:

> Do not deny the love merely because your mode of expression is quieter.

## Sumire → Wien

> 「諦めなければ、何も終わらないから」
> “As long as you don’t give up, nothing ends.”

Wien answers:

> 「諦めるわけないでしょ！」
> “There’s no way I’d give up!”

This is the most obvious temperament match.

Sumire and Wien are both girls whose ambition survived repeated refusal:

-   background roles and failed recognition for Sumire;

-   academy rejection and family hierarchy for Wien.


Sumire’s mature principle is not that everyone will eventually be chosen.

It is that refusal does not have to author the ending.

Wien answers in exactly the forceful register with which she should inherit that idea.

## Chisato → Mei

> 「足を止めちゃダメだよ」
> “Don’t stop moving.”

Mei answers:

> 「最後まで走り続けてみせる！」
> “I’ll keep running until the very end!”

Chisato’s entire life is built around bodily persistence:

-   dance;

-   training;

-   becoming strong;

-   structuring support;

-   and turning attachment into movement.


Mei, the new club president, receives a leadership principle stated through the body.

Her response retains her rough force, but it is no longer defensive. It is a vow.

## Ren → Natsumi and Tomari

> 「夢をみることを忘れないで」
> “Do not forget how to dream.”

They answer:

> 「絶対に忘れないですの！」
> 「絶対に忘れません！」

This is the most thematically loaded pairing.

Natsumi once believed repeated failure had revoked her right to dream.

Tomari treated dreams as dangerous, irrational exposures likely to hurt Natsumi again.

Ren—whose own life was once trapped inside a misread inherited dream—tells them to keep dreaming without allowing the dream to become rigid doctrine.

The sisters answer in their distinct registers:

-   Natsumi’s emotional, branded 「ですの」;

-   Tomari’s formal 「ません」.


They remain different while making the same promise.

---

# XXV. The final song: the first Liella! song becomes the last anime song

The five graduates announce:

> 「これが、私たち３年生の、スクールアイドルとして歌う最後の歌」
> “This is the last song we third-years will sing as school idols.”

Then:

> 「私たちの、そして、みんなにおくる…始まりの歌！」
> “A song of beginning—from us, and for everyone!”

The song is:

> **「始まりは君の空 ～11 Ver.～」**

At the franchise level, this is Liella!’s original debut song.

Within the anime, it functions as the song the founding five create at school before graduation.

That double position is perfect:

> Liella!’s first real-world song becomes its final television-anime song.
> The song of beginning becomes a graduation song.
> The five-member song becomes an eleven-member song.

The series does not close with a newly written song about endings.

It returns to beginning.

---

# XXVI. Why 「始まりは君の空」 is the correct final song

The lyrics begin:

> 「なにか目覚めそうだよ」
> “Something feels as though it may awaken.”

> 「まだ名もないキモチが」
> “A feeling that does not yet have a name…”

This was the language of girls before their future became legible.

At graduation, the lines acquire a different meaning. The original five now know what awakened:

-   Kanon’s recovered voice;

-   Keke’s vocation;

-   Chisato’s leadership;

-   Sumire’s right to center;

-   Ren’s living inheritance;

-   Liella!;

-   Yuigaoka’s school-idol tradition.


But the younger members remain inside the condition of unnamed future feeling.

The song therefore speaks simultaneously from:

-   remembered beginning;

-   present farewell;

-   and future possibility.


## 「あとは勇気ときっかけ」

> “All that remains is courage and an opportunity.”

Keke was Kanon’s opportunity.

Kanon became an opportunity for the others.

The continuing Liella! will become an opportunity for people the founders have never met.

The mechanism outlives the origin.

## 「一歩目を一緒に飛ぼうよ」

> “Let’s take that first leap together.”

This is the series in one line.

*Superstar!!* repeatedly insists that first steps are relational:

-   Kanon and Keke;

-   Kanon and Chisato;

-   Mei and Shiki;

-   Natsumi borrowing Liella!’s dream;

-   Wien singing with Kanon after the goal became impossible;

-   Tomari entering to understand Natsumi;

-   the new six accepting responsibility together.


## 「手をつないでおけば…怖くない！」

> “As long as we hold hands…we aren’t afraid!”

Episode 11 complicated this principle by showing Kanon that visible physical support cannot be the only condition under which she can sing.

The finale gives the mature interpretation:

> Holding hands does not always mean physical presence.
> It can mean carrying words, memories, practices, and relationships inside oneself.

---

# XXVII. The vocal structure: five voices open into eleven

The arrangement initially foregrounds the original five.

The familiar pairings are preserved:

-   Chisato and Ren;

-   Sumire and Keke;

-   Kanon as the organizing central voice.


The first section allows us to hear the founding group after three years of development. The song’s youthful optimism is no longer naïve. The same words now pass through voices that have experienced:

-   defeat;

-   expansion;

-   family conflict;

-   rivalry;

-   separation;

-   championship;

-   and graduation.


The bright melodic language remains, but the emotional knowledge underneath it has deepened.

Then the arrangement expands.

## Kinako receives the sky

Kinako sings:

> 「空に描くのは」

Kanon and Kinako then sing together:

> 「どんな どんな夢か教えて」

This is the central vocal handoff.

Kanon does not disappear before Kinako sings.

They overlap.

Succession is represented not as replacement but as temporary unison:

> the old center and emerging successor share a line until the new voice is securely inside the song.
